# Dogfood testing tracker

> **Live state — maintained automatically, without asking** (standing rule in
> `.claude/CLAUDE.md`, added 2026-08-15). Every session updates this file when:
> a user-facing feature merges (add it as UNTESTED), Dan reports a hand-test
> result (move it to TESTED or FOUND-A-BUG with the ticket number), or a fix
> for a found bug ships (move it back to UNTESTED for re-verification).
> When Dan asks "what should I test?", answer FROM this file: the UNTESTED
> and RE-TEST sections, phrased as steps + expected result.

Previously 2026-10-02 (fourth pass) — **⭐⭐⭐ GIT V2 IS DONE: ALL FIFTEEN ITEMS, and the Changes tab can now STAGE, DISCARD AND COMMIT.** The top five rows are the write half plus the two surfaces that finish the read half. ⚠️ **USE A SCRATCH REPO for the top rows** — they change your repository, and one of them (discard on an untracked file) destroys work nothing else has a copy of.

**IF YOU ONLY DO FOUR THINGS, DO THESE:**

1. **Press ↶ on a row and say NO to the dialog.** Nothing at all should happen. (#1049 step 5)
2. **Put a failing `pre-commit` hook in a scratch repo and commit.** It should STOP the commit and show the hook's own words — and that is a deliberate decision I want your view on, because everywhere else switchboard refuses to let a repo run anything. (#1050 step 7)
3. **Make the history diverge and press ↓.** It must REFUSE and change nothing rather than starting a merge there is no screen here to finish. (#1052 step 5)
4. **Open a session on a SUBFOLDER of a repository** and check the per-file `+/−` numbers are there. They used to vanish silently. (#1043 step 11)

Previously 2026-09-28 — **⭐ v0.8.100 IS OUT, AND THAT UNBLOCKS SEVENTEEN ROWS AT ONCE.** Nothing below was testable this morning: the last release was v0.8.99 on 2026-09-23 and **nineteen feature commits** had piled up behind it — the whole of **E13 (Dispatch, #946–#951)**, which carries Phase 2's exit criterion 5 and has never been exercised by hand; **#952**, the Terminal transport's removal; **E22 complete** (#972, #973, #974); and #953, #581, #704, #828, #856, #886, #903, #947, #967, #977.

**Install v0.8.100 before anything else in this file.** The rows are ordered for one sitting: #952 first, because everything after it assumes the approval behaviour it changed; then the approvals work; then the subagents; then Dispatch, which is the largest block of genuinely unexercised product here; then the singles; then the four RE-TEST rows for things you reported yourself.

Also 2026-09-28 — **a FOURTEENTH row, and it is something that has been QUIETLY MISSING for a month.** #977: when a session hands work to helpers (sub-agents), their part of the conversation vanished — a silent gap, then the main reply carrying on as if nothing had happened. The captions and the indenting were built and working the whole time; nothing was feeding them. One switch was doing two jobs. Step 4 of the row below is the half that never worked on this transport at all: a resumed session getting its helpers' history back.

Also 2026-09-28 — **a THIRTEENTH row, and step 4 of it is something that has never worked.** #974 adds **Approve all in this file** — approve a change and stop being asked about that one file — and, far more importantly, **a way to take a standing approval back**. **“Allow all (this session)” has had no undo since it shipped**: one click handed the session blanket approval until it restarted, with no surface anywhere to reverse it. The card's ⋯ menu now lists what is standing and lets you cancel any of it.

Also 2026-09-28 — **a TWELFTH row, and it is the one to argue with.** #973 ships **Deny with feedback**: refusing a request and telling the session why, in your own words. The field is the small half. The half worth your judgement is what the session DOES with it — and there is history here: a denial that reads like infrastructure makes Claude announce it is blocked and go looking for another route (that was #94, and the wording that fixed it had quietly been deleted along with the terminal transport, so every denial since #952 went out with the bad text). Step 2 of the row below is the only check that the real CLI behaves.

Also 2026-09-28 — **an ELEVENTH row, and it is one of YOURS.** #967 — "when it's
doing a lot of things it doesn't always keep the session scrolled to the bottom" — is
fixed. The cause was a click: expanding a tool box or pressing Copy inside the
conversation was briefly treated as if you had scrolled away, and while output was
pouring in that was enough to unstick the view. ⚠️ **The e2e cannot reproduce this bug**
(the fake emits a whole turn in one tick, so the pin always catches up), so the hand-test
below is the only check that it works against a real streaming CLI. The rule itself is
unit-tested 18 ways, including against the old behaviour side by side.

Also 2026-09-27 — **a TENTH row, and it is a KNOWN MISS rather than a feature.**
Porting #972 onto a real diff surfaced that the prompt box renders ~49px taller than the
room `roomForBox` offers it, so the conversation's promised 60px floor is not held once a
permission bar is docked in a short window (**#981**). The arithmetic predates #972; the
taller bar is what made it reachable. What IS fixed and tested is the part that matters:
the answer buttons never leave the screen. **FIXED 2026-09-30 (#981) - the top row of
UNTESTED replaces this and the RE-TEST row below.** The arithmetic was never wrong: the
bar is SQUEEZED while the box is tall, so the room was measured against a bar 72px shorter
than the one that then sprang back into it.

Also 2026-09-27 — **a NINTH row, and it is the first FEATURE row in a while
rather than a consequence of the deletion.** #972 put a real diff in the approval card:
colour, syntax highlighting, several changes shown separately in apply order, and
nothing silently trimmed at 1,500 characters any more. Two things in it are worth your
judgement rather than a tick — a layout control that deliberately refuses you on a
narrow card, and a diff that gives up its own height so the prompt box survives a short
window.

Also 2026-09-27 — **an EIGHTH row, and it is a FIX rather than a gap.** The

The row below is worth doing in the same sitting as the two above it: all three came

## Untested — never exercised by hand

| What | How to test | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **A DOCUMENT SAYS WHEN IT HAS STOPPED UPDATING (#506)** | **MERGED 2026-10-10 — NOT RELEASED; it will be in 0.8.119.** **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: open ONE session in a folder that has a Markdown file in it, and open that file from the session (click its name in the conversation, or the Files tab). Close the session (the ✕ on its tab, then OK). **Expected: within about two seconds a strip appears across the top of the document: "No longer following this file. No open session works in its folder any more..." with a Follow again button. The document itself is still there and still readable.** **(2)** Edit the file in another editor and save. **Expected: the document does NOT change. That is what the strip is telling you.** **(3)** Press **Follow again**. **Expected: the strip changes to "Still not following this file" and says to open a session in that folder or use File › Open File….** **(4)** Open a new session in the SAME folder (+ session). **Expected: the strip disappears by itself, and the document now shows the edit you made in step 2.** Save another edit. **Expected: it appears; the document is live again.** **(5)** Do steps 1 and 2 again, then use **File › Open File…** and pick that same file. **Expected: no second tab; the document you already had open loses its strip and shows your edit.** **(6)** NEARBY: open TWO sessions in one folder, open a file from one, close that one. **Expected: no strip; the document keeps updating, because the other session still works there.** **(7)** NEARBY: delete a file a document has open. **Expected: the old "This file has been deleted or moved" strip, exactly as before, and not the new one.** | **The automated tests cover:** steps 1 to 5 end to end in the real app (two tests), steps 6 and 7 at unit level, and that a link followed out of a stopped document starts clean. **They do NOT cover:** how the strip reads and looks to you (the wording is long: say if it should be shorter), a document in a popped-out window, and a file on a network drive. **A choice I made:** Follow again cannot grant access by itself. Only an open session over the folder, or you picking the file, does that; a button in the page that widened what the app may read would be the wrong way round. |
| **A SESSION MOVED INTO A GROUP LANDS AT THE BOTTOM OF IT (#582)** | **MERGED 2026-10-09 — IN v0.8.118, installable now.** **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: open three sessions, one after another. Make a group and put the FIRST and the THIRD in it. Now move the SECOND one in (drag its row onto the group, or right-click ▸ Move to group). **Expected: it is the LAST row in the group. (It used to appear between the other two, because that is the order they were opened in.)** **(2)** Do the same with the sessions across the top: drag a pill onto a group, then open the group's list. **Expected: it is last there too.** **(3)** Restart the app. **Expected: the same order.** **(4)** Move a PINNED session into a group. **Expected: it goes to the front with the other pinned ones; that rule did not change.** **(5)** NEARBY: drag a session that is in NO group onto the empty part of the Sessions list. **Expected: nothing happens (it used to make a pointless round trip and could make the cards re-arrange).** Drag one that IS in a group there. **Expected: it leaves the group, as before.** | Automated tests cover: the rule (last; the group's existing order kept; a session coming back to an old group is last again; a move that is not a move changes nothing; a pinned one stays first), the empty-list drop doing nothing for a session already in no group, and a real-app test of step 1 through the menu plus step 3. That real-app test was run with the fix switched off and FAILED (the session was in the middle), then passed with it on. **NOT covered:** step 2 on the strip by a real drag (it goes through the same one function); step 4 in the real app. **A limit, on purpose:** moving a session OUT of every group does not choose where it lands among the loose sessions; that order is the app's list of sessions, as before. |
| **⭐ DRAG A STACKED SESSION TO THE SIDE FOR A FULL-HEIGHT COLUMN (#731)** | **MERGED 2026-10-09 — IN v0.8.118, installable now.** Your report: two sessions stacked on one side, one beside them, and a stacked one could not be given its own full-height column. **What I found: the drop existed, but you had to let go within about 8 pixels of the edge of the workspace, and its marker was a 20-pixel sliver. From 12 pixels in you got the nearest card's own split, which is the "only above or below" you kept getting.** **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: three sessions, two stacked on the left and one on the right. Drag the BOTTOM-left session's tab toward the far left of the workspace and stop about a finger-width in from the edge. **Expected: a tall blue block down the whole left side, top to bottom. Let go: that session is now its own full-height column on the far left.** **(2)** Do the same with a stacked session toward the far RIGHT. **Expected: a full-height column on the far right.** **(3)** Drag a tab over the middle-left of a card (well in from the edge). **Expected: that card's own left half lights up, as before: this splits that one card.** **(4)** NEARBY, AND IMPORTANT: drag a tab onto another card's ROW OF TABS in the top row. **Expected: it still docks there as a tab. (Widening the edge strip broke exactly this until I narrowed the top edge again; a test caught it.)** **(5)** ONLY YOU CAN JUDGE: is a finger-width the right depth? Too easy to hit by accident when you meant to split the outermost card? Too hard still? | A real-app test drags a real tab with the mouse: 30 pixels in from the left edge the marker is the full height of the workspace and at least a fifth of its width, and the drop makes a full-height far-left column; the same on the right; further in, a card's own split is still offered; the top edge strip is still thin. Unit tests cover the rule. The side strips are also thin where they cross a row of tabs, so dropping a tab "before the first tab" of the left-hand card still works (found in review, tested). **NOT covered:** step 5; the top and bottom edges by a drop (only their marker was checked); a popped-out window. **A limit of the docking library, said plainly:** it takes ONE depth for all four edges. I set 40 pixels and then refuse its top and bottom marker unless the pointer is within its original 10, which is its own supported hook. If a future version changes that hook, the tab-row test (step 4) is what will go red. |
| **DROP A TAB STRAIGHT ONTO ANOTHER CARD'S ROW OF TABS (#620)** | **MERGED 2026-10-09 — IN v0.8.118, installable now.** One of yours. ⚠️ **WHAT I FOUND, SAID PLAINLY: the drop itself ALREADY WORKED in 0.8.117.** I drove a real mouse drag in the real app before changing anything: dropping a tab on another card's tab, or on the empty part of its row of tabs, docked it there. The docking library does it. **What was wrong was that you could not SEE it:** the "it will land here" marker was a pale grey on a pale grey row, invisible in a screenshot of the drag. So this change makes the marker blue, and adds a test so it cannot quietly stop working. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: with two sessions side by side, drag one session's tab onto the OTHER session's tab and hold it over the right half. **Expected: the right half of that tab turns blue with a blue line on its right edge. Let go: the dragged session is now a tab just after it, in the same card.** **(2)** Do it again onto the LEFT half. **Expected: blue on the left half, a line on the left edge; it lands just before.** **(3)** Drag a tab onto the EMPTY part of another card's row of tabs. **Expected: a blue patch there; it lands at the end.** **(4)** Drag a tab over the middle of a card, then toward its left or right side. **Expected: the old drops still work (stack as a tab, or split the card), and where it will land is now blue instead of grey.** **(5)** ONLY YOU CAN JUDGE: is the blue easy to see without being loud, in the theme you use? **And: was your original trouble that it did not work, or that you could not tell it would? If it truly did not dock for you, tell me exactly where you let go, because I could not make it fail.** | A real-app test drags a real tab with the mouse: right half lands after, left half lands before, the empty row appends, the marker is drawn on the right half and is not transparent, and no session is lost or doubled. | **⭐ YELLOW AND ORANGE MEAN "A SESSION NEEDS YOU", AND NOTHING ELSE (#1165)** | **MERGED 2026-10-09 — IN v0.8.117, installable now.** Your rule. **(1)** Open the app after updating. **Expected: any session that was amber is now indigo (a blue-violet), any that was orange is now magenta. Any group that was orange or olive-yellow is now indigo or purple. Nothing else about them changed.** **(2)** Start a session in a brand-new workspace. **Expected: its colour is indigo. It used to be the exact yellow of "needs input".** **(3)** Make a session ask you something, or ask permission. **Expected: THAT is yellow (a question) or orange (permission): its row, the panel asking, the "N need you" count. This is the only yellow on screen.** **(4)** Look at your own messages in a conversation. **Expected: a plain grey tint and a grey dot, not a yellow one.** **(5)** In Source Control, look at a modified file's "M", the unpushed-commits count, and (if you have one) the "detached HEAD" label. **Expected: none is yellow. M is indigo; the other two are blue.** **(6)** Cause a failure you can see, for example search a conversation for a broken pattern like `(`. **Expected: the message is red, not yellow.** **(7)** A session with a message from another session waiting. **Expected: the small count on its row and tab is blue, not yellow.** | **The automated tests cover the rule itself:** a test now fails if a yellowish colour is added anywhere but the four "needs you" colours, if a palette gains one, or if a new part of the app starts painting with them. They also cover (1) from a saved file. **Nothing checks how it LOOKS**, so this whole row is your eye. **Decided while building, say if any is wrong:** (a) **"Yellowish" is written down as a hue range, 20 to 70 degrees.** That includes ORANGE, because "needs permission" is orange and is a session needing you. (b) **Failures are red; things worth noticing that are not failures are BLUE** (unpushed commits, a connector still connecting, "provider degraded", a file that was deleted while open, the "built from uncommitted changes" marker, the dot that says a session has standing approvals). Blue is also the "working" colour. If you want a separate colour for "notice", say so. (c) **A message from another session waiting to be read is blue, not yellow.** The app already kept those out of the "N need you" count on the reasoning that it is something to read, not a session blocked on you. If you think of it as "needs me", it should go back to yellow and into the count. (d) **Inside the question panel everything stays yellow** (the selected answer, "skipped"), because the whole panel is a session asking you. (e) **Your own messages lost their yellow tint** for a plain one. (f) The two warning strips across the top of the window ("Claude Code is not installed", "several sessions hit errors") are RED now, not orange. (g) Indigo sits between blue and violet, and magenta between violet and pink. With yellow and orange gone there are fewer far-apart colours to choose from; if two sessions look too alike, say which. |
| **⭐ THE EFFORT BUTTON BESIDE THE MODEL NAME (#1115)** | **MERGED 2026-10-09 — IN v0.8.117, installable now.** One of yours: *"how hard the model works… The button goes at the bottom, next to the button to switch the model."* **(1)** Open a session and look under the prompt box, right of the model name. **Expected: a button reading "effort: medium" (or whatever your Claude Code default is).** **(2)** Click it. **Expected: a short list: low, medium, high, extra high, max.** Pick **high**. **Expected: the button reads "effort: high" at once.** **(3)** THE ONE ONLY YOU CAN DO: send the same hard question at **low** and again at **max**. **Expected: max visibly thinks longer. This is the one thing the automated tests cannot check; see the note.** **(4)** Click the model name and switch to **Haiku**. **Expected: the effort button disappears (Haiku 4.5 has no effort levels).** Switch back to Sonnet or Opus. **Expected: it is back, on the level you chose.** **(5)** Close the app and open it again. **Expected: the button comes back on the level you chose, not on medium.** **(6)** On a narrow card (two side by side), look at the row under the prompt box. **Expected: nothing is cut off; the row wraps to a second line if it has to.** | **The automated tests already cover (1), (2), (4), (5) and (6)** in the real app against a stand-in for Claude Code, and the contract was measured against your real Claude Code 2.1.288 without sending a prompt. **NOT verified by anything: that a higher level actually changes how the model answers.** What is verified is that Claude Code REPORTS the level as applied. Seeing the effect costs real turns at each level, so it is step (3), yours. **Things to know:** (a) the ticket assumed a control that no longer exists in your Claude Code (`set_thinking_level`); the working one was found by asking the real program, and is written up in `spike/findings/1115-effort.md`. (b) Claude Code says "success" to a level it does not know and changes nothing, so the app sets the level and then READS IT BACK; a level that did not take leaves the button where it was with a note beside it. (c) It is a plain drop-down, not a hand-built menu like the model's. (d) The level is remembered per session card and put back when the session starts again, because Claude Code itself forgets it when the session stops. (e) **While a session is working the button is dimmed**: nobody has measured what Claude Code does with a new level halfway through a turn, so it waits for the turn to end. Say if you want that measured (it costs a few real turns). (f) **The level is put back when the card's conversation is next on screen**, not the instant the session restarts: a session that restarts while its card is folded away runs on the default until you look at it. (g) The same probe found that Claude Code can be ASKED which model a session is on (the app currently waits for the first reply to find out, which is why a fresh card says "model?"). Not changed here; worth a ticket. |

(d) **"Even" is per divider**: one card on the left and two stacked on the right gives left and right half each. While it is working, click its 🕘 history button and pick an older conversation. **Expected:** the list stays open and asks — *This session is working. Opening that conversation here stops it, and what it is doing right now is lost.* — with **Cancel** and **Stop it and open that conversation**. **(2)** ⚠️ **Press Enter straight away.** Cancel is the selected one, so nothing should happen except the question going away: the session is still working, the list is still open. **This is the safety of the whole thing — try to trip it.** Pick with the keyboard (arrow to a row, Enter) and keep Enter held, or press it twice quickly. **DOUBLE-CLICK a row, and triple-click one**, on different parts of the row. It must never stop the session: the stop button cannot be pressed for the first moment after the question appears (it is greyed), and the question names the conversation it is about. **(3)** Pick again and click **Stop it and open that conversation**. The session stops, the card opens the conversation you picked, and no "session ended" overlay appears. **(4)** Go back to the history list: the conversation you were pulled out of is there, with everything up to the moment you stopped it. **(5)** Do (1) with a session that is waiting on a permission prompt or a question: the wording changes to say the question is left unanswered. **(6)** Escape while the question is up: it answers "no" and the list stays open. | UNTESTED. ⚠️ **Step 2 is the one that matters** — the tests check where the focus is and that Enter lands on Cancel, but a held key and a real keyboard are yours to try. ⚠️ **Step 5 is the least certain corner:** a permission prompt that was showing when the session was stopped should disappear with it; if a toast or an approval bar for the dead session lingers, I want to know. Covered by tests, don't repeat: that the "yes" lifts ONLY the busy refusal (a conversation another card holds, or one that is not there, still leaves the session untouched), that only a literal yes counts, Cancel-has-the-focus, Escape, and the whole stop-and-open end to end with a session that never finishes its turn. |

(#1013)** | **IN v0.8.111 (2026-10-03) — installable now.** This is the one row a machine cannot answer. **(1)** Open the 6–8 sessions you normally run and get several writing long replies at once — the state #1013 was captured in. **(2)** While they stream, TYPE in a composer and DRAG a panel. **Expected: noticeably less lag than v0.8.110.** Not necessarily none. **(3)** Watch a long reply arrive. **Expected: it still streams in smoothly** — updates land 20 times a second rather than per word, and it should not look choppy. That number, before and after, is the verdict. | Unit tests prove the count (4,001 → 1,335 messages and 32 → 11 MB for a 16,000-character reply), that a stopped block is told at once, that a held update never overwrites the finished message, and that none survives a clear. **No test measures how the app FEELS**, and the probe says so: it counts messages, not CPU. If this is not enough, the next step is already measured (send only the new text — 184× fewer bytes) and filed. |
| **⭐⭐⭐ FETCH, PULL, PUSH AND BRANCH — the counts have buttons at last (#1052)** | **IN v0.8.110 (merged 2026-10-02) — installable now.** ⚠️ **Use a scratch repo with a REAL remote you do not mind pushing to** (a throwaway GitHub repo, or `git clone --bare` somewhere local and point at that). **(1)** Open a session on it → **Changes**. **Expected: a ⟳ beside the branch line.** Press it: nothing visible should change on a repo that is level, and that IS the success case. **(2)** Commit something locally. **Expected: an ↑ appears beside the `↑1` count.** Press it. **Expected: the commit is on the remote** — check on the other side. **(3)** Push something to the remote from ELSEWHERE, then press **⟳** here. **Expected: a ↓ appears with the count.** Press it: **expected the commits arrive.** **(4)** ⭐ **Check which buttons exist when there is nothing to do.** **Expected: ↓ and ↑ ABSENT, not greyed** — only ⟳ is always there. **(5)** ⭐⭐ **MAKE THE HISTORY DIVERGE**: commit locally AND push a different commit from elsewhere. Press **↓**. **Expected: it REFUSES, says so in git's words ("Not possible to fast-forward"), and changes NOTHING** — no merge started, no conflict markers, same branch. ⚠️ **This is the design of the button and I want your view:** a normal `git pull` would merge or rebase, and either can stop halfway — leaving a one-click button having started something you must finish, with no screen here for finishing it. **(6)** ⭐⭐ **Make a NEW branch and press ↑.** **Expected: it fails, says the branch has no upstream, AND A SECOND ↑ APPEARS** — *"Publish this branch to the remote"*. Press that: **expected it publishes.** ⚠️ The point is that switchboard did NOT publish a branch because you pressed Push once. **(7)** Watch the buttons DURING a slow push/pull. **Expected: they all disable while it runs.** A second press would be a second push. **(8)** ⭐ **History** tab → any commit row → **⑂**. **Expected: it asks for a name, mentioning that commit's short hash, and starts the branch THERE** (check `git log -1`). **Expected: it does NOT also expand the commit's file list.** **(9)** Cancel the name prompt, and separately type only spaces. **Expected: no branch either time.** **(10)** Try to make a branch called `--force` or `a..b`. **Expected: refused, with a message saying what IS allowed.** **(11)** Make a branch that already exists. **Expected: git's own "already exists" on screen.** **(12)** ⚠️ **If your remote needs a password**, check that pressing ⟳ does not HANG. **Expected: either your credential helper answers silently (normal) or it fails with a message** — never a spinner for ever. | UNTESTED. ⚠️⚠️ **STEPS 5 AND 6 ARE BOTH DESIGN DECISIONS RATHER THAN CORRECTNESS CHECKS, and both are mine to be overruled on.** Pull is `--ff-only` so the button cannot half-happen; publish is a separate press so a branch is never published by accident. ⚠️ **STEP 12 IS THE ONE I CANNOT TEST FOR YOU.** `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` is what stops git waiting for a password on a terminal that does not exist — measured against an unreachable host (fails in under a second, no hang) — but your real remote with your real credential helper is the case that matters. ⚠️ **AND A DELIBERATE OMISSION: there is NO force-push, not even behind a menu.** It can destroy commits on the remote that exist nowhere else, possibly somebody else's, and no confirmation box makes that safe. That one stays in the terminal. **Covered by tests, don't repeat:** 15 on the rules — including that a branch name starting with a dash is refused (the class alone accepts `--all`, because `-` has to be IN it for `feature/e24-git-v2`), that `..` is refused because it would turn one ref into a range, that `checkout` ends with `--` so a branch name can never be read as a pathspec, and that push NEVER carries a force flag · **12 against REAL git with a REAL remote** — a bare repository on disk — where a push is verified by reading the REMOTE's own log, a pull brings down a commit a second clone made, and the diverged case is shown to refuse with no merge started and the other side's file still absent · 11 on the renderer seam · 7 on the header buttons, including that they disable in flight and that publish appears only after git has said that is the gap · 6 on the ⑂, including that it sends the FULL forty-character sha while the prompt shows the short one. **What no machine checked:** whether ⟳ ↓ ↑ in that corner are findable, and whether a refused pull reads as "switchboard is being careful" or as "switchboard is broken". |
| **⭐⭐ STAGE PART OF A FILE — the ⊞ on a changed row (#1051)** | **IN v0.8.110 (merged 2026-10-02) — installable now.** **(1)** In a scratch repo, make a file with TWO separate changes far apart (change line 1 and line 40). **Changes** tab → hover that row → **⊞**. **Expected: a list at the bottom of the pane, ONE LINE PER PART**, each saying where it starts and how much it changes — *"From line 1 — +1 −1"*. **(2)** Press **＋** beside the FIRST part only. **Expected: the file now appears under BOTH *Staged changes* AND *Changes*.** Confirm with `git diff --cached` (only the first change) and `git diff` (only the second). **(3)** ⭐⭐ **CHECK THE FILE ON DISK.** **Expected: completely unchanged — both your edits still there.** Partial staging only touches what git has RECORDED as ready; that is why it has no confirm, unlike ↶. **(4)** **Expected: the parts list reloaded itself** after the ＋ (one part left, not two). Staging one part moves the others, so a stale list would offer to stage something that is no longer where it says. **(5)** Press **⊞** on a file with ONE change, stage it, and look at the list. **Expected: "Nothing left to stage in this file"** — a sentence, not an empty box. **(6)** Check which rows HAVE a ⊞. **Expected: only unstaged, already-tracked files.** A brand-new (untracked) file must NOT have one — git has never seen it, so it has no parts. A staged row must not either. **(7)** Press **⊞** on one file, then **⊞** on a different one. **Expected: the list switches to the new file** and says that file's name — never the old file's parts. **(8)** Press **⊞** again on the same row, and separately the **✕** in the list. **Expected: both close it.** **(9)** Stage a part, then commit. **Expected: the commit contains only that part.** | UNTESTED. ⚠️ **STEP 3 IS THE SAFETY CLAIM and it is the one I would most like confirmed on your machine** — `git apply --cached` is measured never to write the working tree, and the test asserts the bytes on disk, but this is the feature where "it quietly edited my file" would be worst. ⚠️⚠️ **AND A SCOPE REDUCTION YOU SHOULD KNOW ABOUT: there is no LINE-BY-LINE picking.** The item's title said "hunk + selection". Selection was built, and a test against real git caught it **staging the WRONG LINES while `git apply` accepted the patch** — the index came out holding `two/ONE/three` for a selection that asked for `ONE/two/three`, because git writes all deletions before all additions and converting an unselected deletion to context lands it in the wrong place. There is no ordering of an edited hunk body that is right for both directions. It needs per-line zero-context hunks instead, which is **#1056**. A partial-staging feature that silently stages the wrong lines is worse than one that only does whole parts, so it was left out. ⚠️ **ALSO A DELIBERATE UI CHOICE:** screen 8 draws `＋ Stage hunk` on a band INSIDE the diff. This is a list BELOW it instead, because injecting interactive bands into Monaco means view zones re-measured on every fold, every layout toggle and every re-diff — and one wrong measurement puts the button next to the wrong change. Say so if the list is awkward; moving it inside is a refinement of this surface, not a different feature. **Covered by tests, don't repeat:** 20 on the patch algebra — including that a trailing empty line is the output's NEWLINE and not a blank context line (getting that backwards added a phantom context line to every hunk and failed six tests at once), that an absent count means ONE, and the two tests that record exactly why selection was dropped · 8 against REAL git, where one hunk is staged and the INDEX, the WORKING TREE and the remaining unstaged change are all three asserted, a reverse-apply unstages it, a bad patch leaves the index byte-identical, and a control proves a repository could have made `git apply` run a program of its choosing before proving it no longer can · 16 on the renderer seam · 11 on the rendered list, including that it reloads after every ＋ and that a refusal is not the same as "no hunks". **And one bug the tests found:** the apply's error was being WIPED by the reload that follows it, so a hunk that failed to stage would have shown no reason at all. **What no machine checked:** whether a list under the diff is a usable way to pick a part, versus wanting the buttons in the code. |

| **⚠️ And a security fix rode in with it, which you cannot see and should know about (#1038)** | **Merged 2026-10-02.** Nothing to test — recorded because it is the kind of thing worth knowing happened. Reading a project's history turned out to be a way a project could make switchboard **run a program of the project's choosing**: two lines in a repository's own git config (`log.showSignature` and `gpg.program`) and git launches whatever they name, once per signed commit, while reporting complete success. Measured, closed, and tested with a control that proves the hole was real before proving it is shut. It is the same threat #776 is about — a session that may edit files getting the app to run a command for it — through a door nobody had looked at, because this is the first part of switchboard that reads commit objects. Two smaller ones alongside it: a project could make a thousand-commit history report *"no commits yet"*, and a single commit message with a long run of trailing spaces could **freeze the whole app for 48 seconds**. | N/A — no user-visible behaviour. Listed so it is on the record rather than only in a commit message. |

That was the worst case and the one your report named. **(3)** Scroll up through the history fast, with the wheel. **Expected: no blank gaps and no blocks flickering into existence.** If you see either, that is this change and I want to know. **(4)** Scroll up to somewhere in the MIDDLE, switch to another session, switch back. **Expected: exactly where you were reading.** **(5)** Drag the splitter to make a long session's pane **narrower**. Expected: the scroll bar stays the right length and the conversation does not jump. **(6)** Expand a long tool output that has its own inner scroll bar, scroll inside it, scroll the message off screen and back. **(7)** Pop a session out into its own window and repeat 1 and 4 there. | UNTESTED. ⚠️ **Steps 6 and 7 are the least covered corners** — nothing automated exercises a nested scroller across a skip, or a popped-out window's feed. **Step 3 is the characteristic failure mode of this technique** and the reason to look for it specifically. ⚠️ **And the honest caveat: everything was measured on THIS desktop.** The improvement is large (the work behind a keystroke went 28.5ms → 7.3ms at 400 messages with the CPU slowed to a quarter speed, and the frame came back inside its budget), but your laptop is the machine that matters and it has VIPRE on it. **#716 stays open until you say so.** Covered by tests, don't repeat: that the scroll height is exact at 60 and 400 messages and again after a resize (mutation-proved — with the old guessed size restored it fails by 3,409px), the whole existing feed family at 43 of 43, and all four CI jobs including Linux, which is where the first attempt at this died. |
| **⭐ The slow-minute lines now say whether it was us (#1031)** | **IN v0.8.110 (merged 2026-10-01) — installable now.** No behaviour changed; this is the instrument, and it is the input to every perf fix after it. **(1)** `Ctrl+Shift+P` → **Show performance summary**. The long-task sentence reads as before; if the window has had a bad moment there is now a second, indented line under it starting **"During the longest stall:"** naming sessions replying, feeds, blocks on screen and heap. **(2)** Make it say something real: open a few sessions, get two or three replying at once, type while they stream, then reopen the summary — the numbers should match what you had open. **(3)** ⭐ **THE ONE I NEED FROM YOU.** In `%APPDATA%\switchboard\logs\switchboard.log` find a recent `cpu heartbeat` line and read **`rssMb`** on the renderer and **`rssTotalMb`**. Compare against Task Manager’s figure for switchboard. **They should be in the same ballpark.** If `rssMb` reads about a THOUSAND times too small, the unit assumption is wrong — Electron’s own typings do not document it, and this is the one place this change guesses a contract. **(4)** Leave the laptop alone for an hour, come back: the next busy line should carry **`idleSec`** in the thousands. Then provoke a stall while you are using it — **`lagIdleSec`** on that line should be small. Those two are what separate "slow while you were there" from noise in every capture from now on. **(5)** **Sleep the laptop and wake it.** Look for **`lagClockGapMs`** on the beat covering the wake. ⚠️ **If it is ABSENT that is a real finding, not a failure** — see the note. **(6)** Typing should feel exactly as it did; this change deliberately puts no code on the keystroke path. | UNTESTED. ⚠️ **Step 3 is the one no machine here could do** and it is the only guess in the change. ⚠️ **Step 5 needs reading honestly:** a gap between the two clocks means wall time the monotonic clock did not see; **agreement means NOTHING.** Across modern standby Windows keeps that counter ticking, so the case that motivated the gauge (your #1003 beats — 3–4s drift, no resume event, laptop only) is the case it is most likely to be blind to. **`spike/probes/1031/probe-clocks-across-sleep.mjs` settles it properly and needs your laptop** — run it, sleep the machine, wake it, paste the WAKE line into #1031. Covered by tests, don’t repeat: all five additions, the two review blockers, and that nothing new can reach a public issue body. |

| **Help ▸ Feature request (#1008)** | **IN v0.8.110 (merged 2026-09-30) — installable now.** ⚠️ **Nothing in the test suite ever presses Send**, so every step below is genuinely unexercised. **(1)** **Help ▸ Feature Request…** — dialog appears, Send greyed out. **(2)** Open it from the palette (`Ctrl+Shift+P`, type *feature*), including with a composer focused — it should still open. **(3)** Leave Title empty, write a sentence in the big box — Send comes alive. **(4)** Pick *Open an email*, press Send: your mail client opens, addressed, subject `[switchboard feature] <your first line>`, text in the body, **and the dialog closes by itself**. Don’t actually send it. **(5)** Re-open, type, pick *Open a ticket*, press Send: the browser opens GitHub’s new-issue page with title and body prefilled, dialog closes. **Don’t press GitHub’s Submit.** Before pressing Send you should see a line saying the tracker is private. **(6)** Open, type, **Escape** — closes, sends nothing; re-open and the box is empty, not holding last time’s words. **(7)** Open **Report a problem**, then **Help ▸ Feature Request…** without closing it — the first closes as the second opens. | UNTESTED. **Steps 4 and 5 are the whole item** and no machine can press them. Covered by tests, don’t repeat: the composed email link and issue URL (prefix, title, body, truncation note, and that an emoji never breaks either — that was a real crash caught in review), both failure paths, the dialog in jsdom, the Help-menu wiring, and on the real app that the item opens the dialog and typing survives. Nothing leaves the machine except by your click — there is no `fetch` in the feature at all. |
| **Shortcuts and palette commands say what they did — or why they cannot (#941, #942)** | **IN v0.8.110 (merged 2026-09-30) — installable now.** Best heard with a screen reader on, but the sentences are observable either way. **(1)** Open a session, click its row in the Sessions list, `Ctrl+Shift+P` → `collapse session` → Enter: the card folds into the strip and you hear *"<name> collapsed to the strip"*. **(2)** Restore it, then `hide session`: the card leaves the workspace and you hear *"<name> hidden, still running — use Go to <name> in the command list to bring it back"*. The session stays in the list and keeps running. **(3)** Follow that route: `Ctrl+Shift+P` → the session’s name → Enter — it comes back to the slot it left. **(4)** With the card hidden, press `Ctrl+Shift+↓`: nothing moves and you hear *"No session is focused"* — previously **silence**. Same for `Ctrl+Alt+P`. **(5)** With no sessions open, `Ctrl+Space` → *"Nothing is waiting on you"*. **(6)** Mistype a shortcut (`Ctrl+Shift+F9`): silent, and the keystroke is not eaten. **(7)** HOLD `Ctrl+Shift+↓` with nothing focused: it says it once, not while held. | UNTESTED. **Two decisions were made for you, each one line to change:** (a) *hidden* names its recovery route, keyed on the RUNG reached rather than the command — so `Ctrl+Shift+↓` landing on hidden says the same sentence; (b) **EVERY** disabled shortcut speaks, not a curated subset, and not gated behind a double-press. ⚠️ **A popped-out window hears NONE of this** (#1022) — the live region is mounted once at the app root. Covered by tests: 40 dispatcher cases, 37 ladder sentences, 5 disabled-wording cases off the real registry, plus an e2e that drives the palette and keyboard in a real window and reads the live region. |
| **The four buttons under the prompt box read as buttons, and the model button is no longer the faded one (#1009)** | **IN v0.8.110 (merged 2026-09-30) — installable now.** **(1)** On **nordic**, look at the row under the prompt box — autonomy, the model, **Compact**, **Clear**. **Expected: four small filled boxes with an edge you can actually see**, where before they were near-invisible outlines. **(2)** **Compare their colour.** Expected: all four written in the same ink — the model button used to be visibly fainter than the other three. **(3)** **Hover each one.** Expected: it lights up (fill and edge both step brighter). **(4)** Switch to **daylight**, **high contrast** and **soft contrast** and repeat 1–3; the treatment should hold on all four themes. **(5)** Get a button into its dead state — press **Compact** and watch it while the compact is going out, or look at the row on a session that has ended. **Expected: it visibly fades and stops responding**, and hovering tells you why. **(6)** Press **Clear**: the confirm **Go** button should still be red-tinted and should NOT light up on hover, while **Cancel** beside it behaves like the others. | UNTESTED. **Step 2 is complaint 1 and steps 1/3 are complaint 2.** Covered by tests, **don’t repeat it**: every contrast ratio on this row is asserted per theme from the token files (edge ≥ 3:1 against the chip and both panels, words ≥ 4.5:1 on the fill, the hover fill measurably different), and the shared treatment is pinned so one chip diverging again reddens. What machines cannot judge is whether it now LOOKS like a button to you, and whether four filled chips are too loud for the row. |
| **The conversation keeps a strip above the prompt box when a permission is waiting (#981)** | **IN v0.8.110 (merged 2026-09-30) — installable now.** You need a **short** session pane: pop a session out and drag the window short, or split the workspace three or four ways. **(1)** Type a long prompt — several paragraphs, enough that the box grows until it stops. **(2)** Now get the session to ask for permission (put it on **ask** and tell it to run a command). When the bar appears, **look at the conversation above the box**. **Expected: a readable strip of it is still there.** Before this it was squeezed to about one line while the prompt box held on to room it had already been told it could not have. **(3)** With the bar still up, press **Deny with feedback…**. The box that opens is tall; **expected: the prompt box gives way for it** rather than ignoring it, and **Allow** and **Deny** stay on screen. **(4)** ⚠️ **KNOWN AND DELIBERATE:** in a pane too short for all three, the conversation is what yields — the prompt box keeps one line and the answer buttons stay reachable. If that reads wrong to you, say so; it is a choice, not a bug. **(5)** Drag the pane taller again: the prompt box should grow back into the room. | UNTESTED. **Step 2 is the bug and step 3 is the half nothing watched for at all.** Covered by tests, **don’t repeat it**: the floor holding when a bar docks under a long prompt, the box giving way when a docked bar grows in place, and both proven by reverting the fix. **What no test can tell you** is whether the strip that survives is ENOUGH conversation to be worth having at that size — 60px is about three lines, and the number was picked long before anyone looked at it on a real short pane. |
| **Sessions can take a note off the shared board (#861)** | **IN v0.8.110 (merged 2026-09-30) — installable now.** Needs **two** sessions in one workspace. **(1)** Ask session **A**: *"use the blackboard to publish a note under `build-status` saying the build is green"*. Then ask **B** to read `build-status`. **Expected: B gets the note and says A left it.** **(2)** Ask **B** to remove that note. **Expected: B confirms it removed it and names who had left it and how big it was** — not just "done". **(3)** ⭐ **THE ONE THAT MATTERS.** Now ask **A** to read `build-status` again. **Expected: A is told the note WAS REMOVED and by which session** — not that it hasn't been written yet. If A tells you to wait for it, stop and say so: that is the exact bug this was built to prevent. **(4)** Ask a session to read a key nobody ever used. It should say nothing is there as an ordinary answer, with **no** mention of anyone removing anything — the two "not found" cases must stay different. **(5)** Ask a session to remove a key that does not exist: ordinary answer, not an error. | UNTESTED. Covered by tests, **don't repeat it**: the whole lifecycle over the real pipe (`check:bus`), the cap genuinely reopening, the remembered-removals list being bounded at 100 and cleared by a republish, and that removing never echoes the note's contents back. **What no test can tell you** is whether an agent actually REACHES for the remove tool when it hits a full board — the refusal now names it, but whether a model acts on that is a judgement only real use makes. |
| **`/usage` no longer eats a reply you interrupted (#978)** | Needs one session on **0.8.102 or later** — it is NOT in 0.8.101. **(1)** Ask for something long (*"count slowly from 1 to 200"*). **(2)** While the answer is still arriving, press **Stop**. Leave the half-finished answer on screen. **(3)** Now type `/usage` and send it. **Expected: the half-finished answer STAYS exactly where it was, and the usage numbers appear BELOW it as a separate reply.** What must not happen: the interrupted answer turning into the usage numbers in place — same position, different text. **(4)** Repeat with `/cost` and `/context`. | UNTESTED. ⚠️ **This one may be hard to trigger and that is worth knowing before you spend time on it.** The overwrite needs the interrupted turn to leave an unfinished reply that Claude Code never closes off, and whether a Stop reliably does that has NOT been measured — the CLI usually does send the closing message, and when it does there is nothing to overwrite. The fix is right either way and is pinned by unit tests; if you cannot make it misbehave in a couple of tries, mark it TESTED-BY-PROXY and move on rather than hunting it |
| **Another session's words can no longer open your files, and a mention folds up (#832 + #830)** | **Merged 2026-09-29 — released long since, installable now.** Needs **two** sessions in **different** folders. **(1)** ⭐ **THE BUG, and you can stage it exactly.** In session **A**'s folder, create a folder called `types` with a couple of files in it. Get session **B** to say the words `@types/node` out loud in its conversation (*"say the literal text @types/node and nothing else"*). Now from **A**, send *"what did `@B` just say?"*. **Expected: A answers, and nothing else happens.** Before this, A's CLI read the `@types/node` inside B's quoted output as a path in **A's** folder and silently attached or listed it. In A's turn you should see the quoted text with a **backslash** in front of it — `\@types/node` — and a line saying file mentions were escaped. **(2)** ⭐ **THE FOLD.** That same turn should show a single **Context from B** row, folded, with **your own question underneath it in full**. Click the row: B's output opens. Click again: it folds. Before this, the whole turn collapsed to one line showing the first 160 characters of **B's** transcript, and your question was hidden behind the expander. **(3)** **THE NEGATIVE TEST, and it is the one worth doing carefully.** Copy the folded row's opening line — the `[Context …]` line — out of the conversation, then type or paste a fake one of your own into the prompt box with some text under it and send it. **It must render as plain text, exactly as you typed it — NOT folded.** If anything you can type or paste folds itself away, stop and tell me: that is the whole forgery guard failing. **(4)** Select some text inside an opened row and let go: it must stay open. **(5)** Mention a **busy** session with a long conversation. The row should still fold, your question should still be visible, and inside the opened row you may see *"the rest of this context is not shown here"* — that is the conversation view's own size limit, not a send that was cut. **(6)** Drag a **context chip** from one session onto another, and separately have one session **send a message** to another: in both, any `@`-word from the other session should arrive escaped the same way. **(7)** ⚠️ **KNOWN AND DELIBERATE:** folding lasts as long as the session's current run. Restart the app, or resume the session, and those older turns show **in full** again — which is correct, not a bug, but say so if it reads badly. | UNTESTED. **Step 1 is the bug and step 3 is the guard** — both are yours. Measured against the real CLI before anything was built (`spike/findings/832-fenced-at-mention.md`, CLI 2.1.280): a file named inside our content fence **was** attached, and one backslash stopped it. Already covered by tests, **don't repeat it**: the escape asserted against the CLI's own extractor (npm scopes, decorators, emails, line-start, the quoted form, CJK boundaries, idempotence), the guard refusing a look-alike / a typed fence / a mismatched ref / a marker quoted mid-sentence, a smuggled marker inside a genuine section, the size-limit case keeping your question, and the whole row through the real renderer including the selection rule. **What no test can tell you** is whether the escaped `\@` reads as a typo rather than as something deliberate (step 1), and whether the folded row is the right amount of information at a glance (step 2). |
| **A session's helpers are back in the conversation (#977)** | **IN v0.8.100** — install it first. **(1)** Give a session something that makes it delegate — *“use a subagent to summarise the README, then tell me what it said”*. Their work should appear **indented behind a dashed line**, opening with a small grey caption like **Subagent · general-purpose**. Before this fix there was a silent GAP there. **(2)** Make it run **two at once** and watch the captions: every time the speaker changes a new caption appears, including when the first one comes back. **(3)** Drop the detail level to **quiet** — the helper chatter and its captions should disappear together. **(4)** Close the card and reopen it (or restart the app and let the session resume): the helpers' history should come back with the rest of the conversation. That half NEVER worked on this transport. ⚠️ **Expect it at the END of the restored conversation rather than back where it happened** — the recordings are read in a different order from the main conversation and putting them in place would mean re-numbering everything already on screen. Say so if that reads badly; it is a choice, not a bug. | UNTESTED. **Steps 1 and 4 are the claims**; step 4 especially, because it is the half #395 flagged and nobody had ever seen work. Covered by tests — don't repeat: that the main conversation is still NOT derived twice, that the subagent files ARE derived, that the blocks arrive stamped with the agent's id and name, and the whole three-runs/two-ids/one-shared-name caption case end to end. |
| **Approve all in this file, and taking a standing approval back (#974)** | **IN v0.8.100** — install it first. **(1)** Put a session on **ask** and ask it to make two small edits to the SAME file. On the first bar press **Approve all in this file**. The second edit should land **with nothing on screen at all** — no bar, no Events row, no sound. **(2)** Now ask it to edit a DIFFERENT file: you should be asked, normally. The grant is one file, not a mood. **(3)** Look at the card's **⋯** button — there should be a small dot on it. Open the menu: **STANDING APPROVALS** lists the file you approved. Press its **✕**, then ask for the same edit again: you should be asked. **(4)** ⭐ **The one that never worked before.** Press **Allow all (this session)** on a bar. Open **⋯** → **STANDING APPROVALS** → **Everything (this session)** → **✕**. The session should go back to asking. Until this item there was **no way to undo that button at all** — it held until the session restarted. **(5)** Set BOTH (a file and the blanket one), then revoke only the blanket one: your file approval should survive. **(6)** Restart the session and check the menu: it should say **None** — grants die with the running session and are never written to disk. | UNTESTED. **Step 1's silence is the claim** and the only one that needs your eyes on a real CLI: covered by tests are that the router answers at the server, that a different file still holds, that a QUESTION still waits for you, that two spellings of one path are one grant, and that the two rungs revoke independently. Don't repeat those. |
| **Deny with feedback — telling a session WHY you said no (#973)** | **IN v0.8.100** — install it first. **(1)** Put a session on **ask** and give it something that edits a file. When the review bar appears, press **Deny with feedback…** — a small box should open under the diff **with the cursor already in it**. **(2)** Type a correction rather than a refusal — *“wrong file, edit the template instead”* — and press **Enter**. Watch what the session says next: it should **take the correction and act on it**, not announce that it is blocked or quietly reach for a different tool. **(3)** Ask for another edit, press **Deny with feedback…**, then press **Esc**. The box should close and the request should still be sitting there waiting. **(4)** Press plain **Deny** on it: still one click, no box, and the session should stop and ask you what you want rather than trying another route. **(5)** Open the **Events** drawer while a session is holding a request — there is a **Deny…** button on the row; it does the same thing without opening the session. **(6)** Make two sessions ask the same thing at once: the shared card above the workspace should have **no** feedback button, only Allow and Deny. | UNTESTED. **Step 2 is the whole point and it is a judgement call no test can make**: the question is whether the real CLI *behaves* differently when it is told why. Everything else is covered — don't repeat it: that the words reach the CLI wrapped in the do-not-route-around framing (asserted against the actual outbound payload), that Enter sends / Shift+Enter newlines / Esc cancels, that the 500-character cap is enforced on main's side, and that the buttons stay on screen with the box open in a short window. |
| **The Terminal transport is gone — what changed that you can feel (#952)** | **IN v0.8.100** — install it first. **Do this FIRST in the sitting.** **(1)** Put a session on **✎ auto-edit** and give it something that does in-folder housekeeping — *"make a directory called scratch, then move README.md into it and back"*. **You should NOT be asked to approve the `mkdir` or the `mv`.** You would have been before. ⚠️ **THIS IS THE ONE TO ARGUE WITH IF IT FEELS WRONG**: switchboard used to add its own stricter layer on top of Claude Code's, and that layer rode on the terminal. If you want the old feel, **ask** mode stops for everything and has not changed — but say so, because the alternative is rebuilding the layer on the control channel. **(2)** Same session, ask it to read a file **outside** the project folder. You will not be asked about that either. **(3)** Look at the title bar: the **🔓 auto-trust / 🔒 ask trust** chip should be **gone**, not greyed out. Nothing else on the bar may have moved. **(4)** Start a session in **plan** mode and give it something that needs a tool: **you should now get the approval bar in-app**, where before plan sessions never asked. Answer it, then confirm plan mode still refuses to write files. **(5)** Anywhere in the app, look for an **Open Terminal** button or a bar saying Claude is waiting "in the terminal" — there should be none, in any state. **(6)** Open the **MCP** panel on a live session and press **Reconnect**: it should reconnect servers over the control channel. On a **suspended** card it should say to restart the session, and not appear to do anything else | UNTESTED. **Steps 1, 2 and 4 are the judgement calls** — all three are approval behaviour, which is the one place "fewer prompts" is not obviously an improvement. Already covered by tests, **don't repeat it**: that no `PreToolUse` hook is registered at all, that the trust question is never answered on your behalf, that nothing renders a handoff bar, and that the whole suite runs green with no terminal anywhere. What no test can tell you is whether the auto-edit change makes the mode feel too loose |

Expected: the command echo, then nothing.~~ **⚠️ THAT STEP WAS WRONG — do the opposite. Type `/usage`, then `/cost`, then `/context`. Expected: each one PRINTS ITS ANSWER in the conversation**, within a couple of seconds. Note that **your command does not appear above the answer** — Claude Code never sends these back, so the numbers arrive on their own. That is correct, not a second bug. **(3)** The step that still matters: go looking for a THIRD thing — anything else in a conversation that used to come from the recording on disk. Resumed history, Edit diffs, Bash output, todo checklists, links, code fences and the Copy button are all covered by tests and should all work — if any of them is blank or missing, that is a new bug and worth stopping for | UNTESTED. **The premise of this row was half wrong and the correction is the interesting part.** #977 was a real regression and is fixed. #978 was not a gap at all: it was filed from a prediction 2h before the change it blamed even merged, and `/usage` has worked on this transport the whole time — what looked like proof was a disabled test measuring the deleted path. Already covered by tests, **don't repeat it**: every rich block renders, the clipboard round trip, the keyboard walk, resumed history, and `/usage` printing its output. Step 3 is the only part no test can do |
| **The approval toast's Allow / Deny buttons (#952, §5.9)** | Needs a version bump like the rest. **(1)** Turn OS notifications ON in Settings (they are off by default). **(2)** Give a session something that needs approval, then **click away to another window before it asks** — the toast only fires when you are not looking at switchboard. **(3)** The toast must carry **Allow** and **Deny** buttons. Press **Allow**: the session should proceed and the toast should go. **(4)** Repeat and press **Deny** instead. **(5)** Repeat once more and click the toast's BODY rather than a button: switchboard should come to the front on the right card, with the question still waiting — a click is a shortcut, never a verdict. **(6)** Repeat and answer it in the app instead: the toast should withdraw itself | UNTESTED. This was BROKEN for every Direct session since #381 and found by porting the e2e off the deleted hook path: the toast went out with no buttons and its click went nowhere. Already covered by tests, **don't repeat it**: that the toast carries a real request id, that the buttons appear only where the OS can carry them, that a verdict from any surface withdraws the toast. What no test can tell you is whether the Windows Action Center behaves — a toast that times out sits there, and pressing Allow on a stale one is the case worth trying |
| **The real diff in the approval card (#972, §5.16)** | Needs a version bump like the rest. **(1)** Put a session on **ask** and give it something that edits ONE part of a file. **Expected: a proper colour diff with syntax highlighting, not two plain text boxes** — and Allow / Deny working with it on screen. **(2)** Press **Side by side** / **Inline**: both should do something, and the pressed one should always be the one you chose. **On a narrow card** two columns are refused and a small grey **Too narrow for two columns** note appears — the pressed button stays on your choice, which is deliberate. Check the same choice now applies in the **Changes** tab. **(3)** Ask it to write a WHOLE file: everything reads as added, with a line count. Ask it to write an EMPTY file: it should say so in words. **(4)** Ask for several changes to one file in one go (*"rename this variable in three places"*). **Expected: separate changes, labelled `change 2 of 3`, in the order they apply** — scroll inside the diff to reach the later ones. **(5)** Shrink the window until it is short: the diff should get SHORTER and the prompt box and its chip must stay on screen. **(6)** Ask for a shell command: **no editor**, just the command in a plain box — there is no diff to show for a command | UNTESTED. **Step 2 on a narrow card and step 5 are the judgement calls**: the first is a control that deliberately refuses you, the second is the diff giving up height so the rest of the card survives. Already covered by tests, **don't repeat it**: that an Edit/Write/MultiEdit gets a real editor and a Bash command does not, apply order, the labels, the height clamp, and that a diff which fails to load leaves the old boxes with the request still answerable. What no test can tell you is whether 180px is enough diff to actually review from |

Re-test the floor once #981 is done | UNTESTED — and deliberately not fixed in #972. The arithmetic predates it; #972's taller bar is what made it reachable. | **A dispatched session nests under its author and closes itself (#951)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-26) — needs a version bump. **Do this LAST in the Dispatch sitting**, after the four rows below: it needs a dispatch and a round-trip to have happened first. **(1)** Dispatch a **Code Reviewer**. In the sessions list the new card should appear **directly under the one you dispatched from, indented, with a small ↳** in front of its name. **(2)** Quit switchboard and relaunch — the indent should still be there. **(3)** Count down the list and press **Ctrl+&lt;n&gt;** for the indented row: it must open **that** card. The numbering has to agree with your eye. **(4)** Take the findings (**Inject findings** on the Events row), then **leave the app alone and watch the reviewer’s card for a minute**. It should close itself about thirty seconds after the inject. ⚠️ **THIS IS THE JUDGEMENT CALL** — does that read as a tidy-up, or as a card vanishing on you? If it is the second, say so: **Settings → Advanced → Finished dispatched sessions** already offers *leave it open* and *close straight away*, and the default is the thing to argue about. **(5)** Dispatch again, **pin** the reviewer’s card, inject, and wait a minute: the pinned card must **still be there**. **(6)** Dispatch again, inject, and immediately **type something at the reviewer and send it**: it must not close under you. **(7)** Dispatch again and **close your OWN card first**, leaving the reviewer: it becomes an ordinary top-level row — no indent, and no ↳ left dangling | UNTESTED. **Step 4 is the only judgement call**; the rest is here because the automated suite proves the rules and cannot judge the feel. Already covered by tests, **don’t repeat it**: the nesting order and depth (including the author-closed, other-group, pinned and cyclic cases), that the numbering counts the painted rows, that a drag cannot tear a child off its author, that Move up/down are offered only when they really move, the whole policy table, that a session still holding findings is never closed, and the real 30-second linger end to end against the real timer. **Worth knowing before you look:** the indent deliberately **disappears** rather than pointing at nothing — pin one of the pair, or get them into different groups, and the ↳ goes, because a pinned row always sorts to the top of its group and switchboard would rather drop the connector than break that. **Five things keep a card open** whatever the setting says: findings you have not taken (including any owed to *that* card, if it dispatched one of its own), a pin, carrying on the conversation, an unsent message in its box, and a crash. And **closing is closing**: there is no archive of dispatched sessions yet (§5.25, Phase 3), though the conversation is still re-openable from history |
| **The findings come back — the dispatch round-trip (#950)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-26) — needs a version bump. **Do this immediately after the Dispatch row below**, in the same sitting, because it starts where that one ends. **(1)** Dispatch a **Code Reviewer** from a session with a real uncommitted change and wait for it to finish (30–60 s). Open **Events** (the tab on the right, or `Ctrl+Space`). A row should appear **on YOUR card, not the reviewer's**, reading *“Code Reviewer ‘Code Reviewer of &lt;project&gt;’ finished and reported back”*, with the first line of the review under it. **(2)** Click **Inject findings**. The row should change to *“findings waiting in this session’s message box”* and the button should go. Open your own card: the review is sitting in the message box as a highlighted block attributed to the reviewer and **has not been sent**. Press Enter and it goes in. **(3)** ⚠️ **THE JUDGEMENT CALL — compare what landed against what is on the reviewer's card.** It should be the reviewer's final answer and nothing else: no *“let me look at the diff”* preamble from before its first tool call, and nothing missing off the front. **(4)** Dispatch again, wait for the row, then **close the reviewer's card before clicking Inject**. The row stays, **Open it** is replaced by *(that session has been closed)*, and **Inject findings** still works — the block that arrives is attributed to an unknown session, which is expected. **(5)** Dispatch and close the reviewer's card almost immediately. You should get a row saying it stopped before it finished, **with no Inject button** — rather than silence. **(6)** Get a dispatch row and a real held permission on the same card at once, then **Dismiss** the dispatch row: the permission row must still be there | UNTESTED. **Step 3 is the one no test can answer**: the app has no way to know whether the extracted turn reads as the review. It was measured against the real CLI four times (`spike/findings/e13-950-review-last-turn.md`) and the last turn was the findings every time, including the run whose plan-mode exit was refused — but that is four samples of a model's choice, not a protocol rule, and a build that changed it would look exactly like this. Automated tests already cover the extraction rules, the row surviving the author's own status changes, every refusal path, dismiss-by-id, and the whole chain end to end with a real session at each end — **don't repeat any of that**. **Also worth knowing before you look:** the row deliberately **does not count the findings**. Four runs of one prompt enumerated four different ways and the one self-reported count in them pointed at the wrong thing, so it shows the reviewer's opening line instead. If you find yourself wanting a number there, say so — but the honest answer is that there isn't one |
| **Where a dispatched session works, said out loud (#949)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-26) — needs a version bump. Do this in the same sitting as the Dispatch row below. **(1)** ⋯ → **Dispatch…**: each role row should end with a faint `· in this folder` after its description, and **below the list** you should see *Runs in C:\Projects\… — so anything it runs, it runs on the files you are working in.* Check the path is right and the line is not cramped or spilling out of the box. **(2)** Click down the three roles in turn — **the rows must not jump**; nothing should shift under the pointer. **(3)** Turn **Fork sessions** OFF in Settings → Advanced and select a role with `"contextPolicy": "full"` (add one to `dispatchTemplates` in `workspace.json` if there isn't one): its red refusal shows, **Dispatch** greys out, and the *Runs in…* line should **disappear** — the app should not tell you where something runs that is not going to run. **(4)** ⚠️ Add a role with `"workspacePolicy": "fresh-worktree"` to `workspace.json`. Its row should read `· wants its own worktree` and, underneath, *Dispatching into a fresh worktree is not available yet — separate checkouts arrive with the git work in a later release…*, with **Dispatch** dead. **It must never quietly start in your folder instead** | UNTESTED. Step 4 is the one that matters: a reviewer told it has an isolated checkout, running tests in the tree you are editing, is the exact surprise this item exists to prevent. Automated tests already cover the refusal keys and their sentences, main re-asking the refusal at dispatch time, the row rendering both policies, the folder line appearing and disappearing for the right roles, main and the renderer agreeing on the folder, and the dispatched session landing in the author's folder end to end — **don't repeat any of that**. Steps 3 and 4 need a hand-edited `workspace.json` because there is still no template editor, which is also why there is no picker for this setting |
| **Hand a session's work to a fresh session in a role — Dispatch (#948)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-25) — needs a version bump. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: on a session with uncommitted changes, click **⋯ → Dispatch…**, leave **Code Reviewer** selected, replace the task line with one real sentence, press **Dispatch**. You should get a second card called *Code Reviewer of &lt;your project&gt;* in a different colour, already working. Open it: its first message is a clean-room handoff carrying **your diff** and your sentence under **What it was asked to do**. ⚠️ **It should finish and report findings without ever asking you anything** — that is the whole premise and it is measured, not hand-confirmed. **(2)** Pick **Doc Writer** instead: the two text boxes should vanish, replaced by one line saying why (that role is briefed, not handed an artifact). **(3)** `Ctrl+Shift+P` → *dispatch*: three entries, one per role, **no keyboard shortcut on any of them**; picking one opens the same box with that role chosen. **(4)** ⚠️ **From a POPPED-OUT card**: ⋯ → Dispatch… should bring the MAIN window forward with the dialog on it, and the new card should land back in the popout you started from. **(5)** Type into the dispatched reviewer and ask it to change something: it should now ask you for permission to leave plan mode like any other session — typing is how the app knows you are there. **(6)** Afterwards, look in `%USERPROFILE%\.claude\plans\`: a reviewer that decided to write its findings down has them redirected there by the CLI. Your repo stays clean; that folder may collect files | UNTESTED. **Step 4 is the most likely thing to be wrong** — the dialog renders in the main window's DOM, and review caught that a renderer's own `window.focus()` does not raise a window on Windows (#571), so this asks main to raise it via a new `app:raiseMain`. Verified by reading, not by hand. **Step 1's "never asks you anything"** rests on a real CLI probe (`spike/findings/e13-948-plan-unattended.md`): a plan-mode review finished unattended in 19 s with zero approval requests — but the same prompt DOES sometimes ask to leave plan mode, and the app now refuses that at once instead of holding it for five minutes. If you ever see a dispatched reviewer sitting on **needs permission**, that is worth a ticket. Step 6 is litter, not a fault — noted because nothing in the app tracks or cleans that folder |
| **⋯ → Fork into a new session actually carries the history now (#801, fixed in #948)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-25) — needs a version bump. ⚠️ **THIS HAS NEVER WORKED**, so treat it as a first test rather than a re-test. Turn **Fork sessions** on in **Settings → Advanced** (experimental, off by default). Take a session that has had a real conversation — several turns, something specific in it. Click **⋯ → Fork into a new session**. A second card appears in the same folder. **(1)** Its feed should show the ORIGINAL conversation's history replayed into it, not an empty session. **(2)** Ask the new session something that only the original conversation would let it answer ("what did we decide about X?"). It should know. **(3)** Check the ORIGINAL card is untouched and still working | UNTESTED. Found while building #948: the fork's intent was written into the new card's panel but **never forwarded to the spawn**, so every fork since #801 shipped started an ordinary blank session in the right folder — it appeared, it worked, and it carried nothing. Main's entire fork path was unreachable from the UI. It survived because the test suite asserted that the menu ENTRY rendered and never looked at what the spawn was asked for; that suite now records the arguments |
| **A compacted session's handoff no longer quotes Claude back at you (#947)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-25) — needs a version bump. Needs a session whose conversation has been **compacted**: Claude says *"This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context"*. Long sessions get there on their own; you cannot really stage it. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: drag that session's **context chip** into another session's prompt box and choose the **summary handoff** option. The **Goal** section must be the prompt *you* typed. Before this it could be Claude's own recap of the conversation — its summary of your work, presented as the thing you asked for. **(2)** In the same handoff, the header should read **Covers: the most recent part of the conversation — there is older history not included here**, not "the whole conversation". A compaction means earlier history exists, and the document now says so. **(3)** THE CONTROL, and the one worth doing: repeat (1) on an ordinary session that has **never** been compacted. It must be unchanged — header says **the whole conversation**, Goal is your opening prompt. If every handoff now claims to be partial, the fix overreached and I need to know. **(4)** Nothing else about the drop dialog was meant to move: the three options, their sizes, and what lands in the composer should all look as they did. | Automated tests already cover: the summary never reaching Goal or the instructions list on either a cut or an uncut transcript, it being absent from the whole rendered document, the coverage claim flipping to partial, an uncompacted transcript still reporting "whole", and the flag being read as a boolean rather than by truthiness. What no test can produce is a REAL compaction on your machine — the fixture is our idea of the shape, the CLI writes the real thing. Everything else in #947 is machinery with no surface until the dispatch button (#948) lands. |
| **The approval bar shows what a Write, multi-edit or notebook change would actually do (#953)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-25) — installable now. You run on `ask`, so every one of these will come to you on its own without being staged. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: in any session, ask Claude to **create a new file** ("write a file called notes.md with three bullet points about this repo"). When the bar appears it should read **Allow Write?**, the path, then a caption like **New contents — 5 lines** and the text it would write. Before this, you saw the path and nothing else. **(2)** Ask it to **change several places in one file at once**. Expect a count (**3 changes**) and each before-and-after pair stacked, red on the left, green on the right. If it wants more than four, you should see the first four and **+2 more changes not shown** — the count at the top must always be the real total. **(3)** THE ONE THAT COULD BITE: do (2) on a big file so several pairs stack, and **make the switchboard window short** before you answer. The pairs should scroll inside their own box and **Allow / Deny must stay reachable**. If the buttons ever go off the bottom with no way to scroll to them, that is the bug I was most worried about — tell me. **(4)** Confirm an ordinary **shell command** and an ordinary **single edit** look exactly as they always did; nothing about those two was meant to move. **(5)** If you can get two sessions to ask for a `Write` at the same moment, the grouped band at the top should show the same contents the card's own bar would. Hard to stage; skip it if it will not happen naturally. | Automated tests already cover: every tool the `ask` setting holds rendering something rather than nothing, the line count (including the trailing-newline case), the cap and its "+N not shown" line, a malformed change keeping its readable siblings, and an unknown tool falling back to a settings dump. What needs your eyes is **how it looks and whether the buttons stay reachable in a short window** — jsdom has no layout engine, so no test in the suite can answer step 3. |
| **The three keyboard shortcuts that rearrange the window now say what they did (#581)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-24) — installable now. ⚠️ **This row cannot be answered without a screen reader running** — NVDA on Windows, same as #828's row. Nothing in CI has one. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: start NVDA, open a session and **click its row in the Sessions list** (not the prompt box — while you are typing, switchboard keeps its hands off every chord, so nothing will fire). Press **`Ctrl+Alt+P`**: you should hear *"<session> pinned"*. Press it again: *"<session> unpinned"*. The pin glyph in the rail should agree with what you heard. **(2)** Same session, press **`Ctrl+Shift+↑`**. It is already full size, so expect *"<session> is already expanded"* and **nothing should move** — announcing the no-op is the point, not a bug. Then **`Ctrl+Shift+↓`**: *"<session> collapsed to the strip"*, and the card folds into the Collapsed row. **(3)** THE REACH LIMIT, and it is the one place a chord is still deliberately mute: with that session still collapsed, press **`Ctrl+Shift+↑`** again. Expect **nothing at all** — it is no longer the session you are in, so nothing was asked. Click its row in the strip to bring it back and the shortcuts reach it again.
