Tell me if that silence reads as broken; #942 is where the general version of that question lives. **(4)** POSITION: open a second session, put both in one group, stand on the bottom one and press **`Ctrl+Alt+↑`** — *"<session> is now 1 of 2 in <group>"*, and the row moves. Press it again: *"<session> is still 1 of 2 in <group>"*. Nothing moves, and it still tells you. **(5)** THE SAME WORDS FROM THE MENU: on that row press **`Shift+F10`**, walk to **Move down** under *Order in this group*, Enter. The sentence should be the same shape as the chord's — they share one string on purpose. **(6)** THE STUTTER CHECK, and it is the one I would drop a feature over: standing on a rail row, `Ctrl+Alt+P` both announces *"<session> pinned"* AND changes that row's own accessible name, which some screen readers read straight after. If it stutters, say so — I would rather remove the pin announcement than have it said twice. **(7)** NOTHING NEW SHOULD BE AUDIBLE OTHERWISE: work normally for a few minutes with NVDA on — switch cards, let a session go idle, change the layout mode, let a background job report. The new region must stay silent; it speaks only for those three chords. | UNTESTED. Automated: 26 unit tests on the sentences through the real ICU chain (every rung driven off `LADDER_ORDER` so a new one cannot ship a missing word), the ladder's wait-for-the-transition behaviour including a command that bails, one that throws, one that lands somewhere else and a session renamed or ended mid-move; 9 on the two alternating regions (present and empty before the first word, never two sentences readable at once, and a MutationObserver proving three queued sentences each reached the DOM); 36 on the bucket naming; and one e2e journey pressing all three chords in a real window and reading the region back. ⚠️ **Review found the load-bearing bug and it is worth knowing what it looked like:** the ladder announcement first watched the STORE for a rung change, which meant a transition that ended WITHOUT moving anything left a listener armed for good — and the next size change from anywhere (the strip row, the card header, a layout mode moving a dozen cards) was then announced as the outcome of a keypress minutes earlier. Step 7 is the step that would catch a relapse. **What no test can judge:** whether NVDA says any of it on your machine (steps 1–4), the stutter in step 6, and whether the silence in step 3 is acceptable. |
| **A background job's report is its own row, not a fake prompt (#704)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-24) — installable now. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: in a session, start work that runs in the background — `run the test suite in the background` usually does it, or set a Monitor on a CI run — and wait for it to report. You should get a bordered **Background task** row with a readable one-line summary. Before this you got a **New prompt** divider over a tinted box full of raw `<task-notification>` XML you never typed. **(2)** Click the row. It should expand to the whole payload: the job's id, the output-file path, and the sentence telling Claude whether to send a push notification. **That sentence must NOT be in the collapsed row** — it is the harness talking to Claude, and in the row it reads as the app telling you what to do. **(3)** THE STATUS WORD, on the right of the row: let one background job succeed and one fail (`exit 1` in a background Bash). The failed one's word should be **red**; a completed one grey. A Monitor firing says **event**. **(4)** Turn the session to **quiet**. The Background task rows should vanish along with the tool boxes; **normal** brings them back. Is that the right call, or would you rather see them even in quiet? — that is a taste question and it is yours. **(5)** `Ctrl+F` for a word that only appears in the EXPANDED part (the task id is the easy one). It should find it, the result row should say **background task** rather than "block", and jumping to it should unfold the row. **(6)** THE ONE THAT MUST NOT HAVE BROKEN, and it is why the detection is not a simple text match: paste a `<task-notification>` block into the prompt box as the very first thing in a message and send it. It must render as **your** prompt, in the tinted pill — not as a background-task row. | UNTESTED. Automated: 17 tests on the recognition rule (both real payload shapes, an origin with no tag, a tag with no origin, a person quoting one — including opening with it, a malformed origin of every shape, the reminder wrapper, and the `subkind` case below), 9 on the derivation, 7 through the real renderer registry, a turn-divider test proving the NEW PROMPT rule no longer fires above one, a search test proving a term only the expander shows is still findable, and an assertion against the repo's captured 4,697-line transcript that its 9 real notifications derive as notices **with the total block count unchanged at 1579**. ⚠️ **Review narrowed the rule using the CLI's own schema, and the fixture could never have caught it:** the `task-notification` origin also covers `scheduled-trigger` (a scheduled routine's real instruction) and `peer-send-message` (another of your own sessions talking) — either one shown as "Background task" would be a confident mislabel. All 9 fixture lines lack that field, so only reading the schema found it. **What no test judges:** step 4's taste question, and whether the summary the CLI writes is actually useful to read at a glance on real jobs — steps 1 and 3 are the ones to watch for that.
| **The `/` and `@` autocomplete talks to screen readers (#828)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-24) — installable now. ⚠️ **This row cannot be answered without a screen reader running** — NVDA is the one that matters on Windows (free, nvaccess.org). Nothing in CI has one, so every step here is yours. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: start NVDA, click into a session's prompt box and type `/`. You should hear that a slash-command list opened **and how many commands are in it** — something like "Slash commands, 12 available". Before this, silence. **(2)** Press **↓** a few times. Each press should read out the command you landed on. Then keep typing letters so the list narrows — arrowing should still read out the right row, not a stale one. **(3)** Press **Tab**, then try again and press **Enter**. Both must behave exactly as they always have; this item was not supposed to change what any key does. **(4)** Do (1)–(3) again with **`@`** and two or three other sessions open. The opening announcement must say it is the **session** list, not the command list — two lists that announce identically would be as useless as no announcement. **(5)** Press **Escape** with a list open: it closes quietly and the screen reader stops offering you rows. **(6)** Hover the mouse over a row while NVDA is running — the announcement should follow the pointer, because hovering is choosing. **(7)** SIGHTED CHECK, nothing should have moved: open both lists and confirm the popup looks pixel-identical to before — same size, same highlight colour, same position above the box. **(8)** THE JUDGEMENT CALL: is "Slash commands, 12 available" the right thing to hear on opening, or would you rather it named the first row too? It deliberately does not — saying both talks over your first arrow key. | UNTESTED. Automated: 20 tests through the real panel contribution — both lists are named listboxes of options, the two names differ, exactly one row is marked at every settled point and the announcement always names that row (including after the list shrinks under the selection), every relation disappears when the list closes by typing *or* by Escape, the mouse moves it too, the live region is mounted empty before it has anything to say, and the ids cannot be forged by a transcript. Two mutations verified red. ⚠️ **The first design was thrown away in review and this is why the row matters:** it put `role="combobox"` on the textarea, which ARIA-in-HTML does not permit on that element at all, and relied on a role change being announced — which screen readers routinely drop. The docs written alongside it promised an announcement nobody had measured. The shipped version uses a live region instead, which is why step 1 is the step. **What no test can judge:** whether NVDA actually says any of it on your machine, and step 8. |
| **A saved GitHub token switches update checking back on (#856)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-24) — installable now. ⚠️ **Only observable with `gh` signed OUT** — if the GitHub CLI is signed in, this already worked and you will see nothing. **(1)** SET THE STAGE: `gh auth logout`, then launch switchboard and do **Help ▸ Check for updates…**. It should say gently that this machine has no credentials for the release list — no error dialog. **(2)** **Help ▸ Report a problem…**, paste a GitHub token into the **GitHub token** box, **Save token**. It should confirm it is saved. **(3)** THE MAIN ONE, and do it **without restarting**: **Help ▸ Check for updates…** again. It should now give a real answer — "up to date" or an offer. That flip with no restart is the entire item; before this it stayed "no credentials" for ever. **(4)** THE HALF THE ISSUE DIDN'T ASK FOR: if it offers an update, press **Update**. The download must run to completion. If only the check had been wired you would get the offer and then a failed download, which is worse than the honest refusal it replaced. **(5)** UNDO: save an **empty** box to forget the token, check again — back to "no credentials". Then `gh auth login` and confirm checking works with no token saved. **(6)** THE THING THAT MUST NOT HAVE BROKEN: this change moved where the credential store is built during startup. If you have **phone push or a webhook** configured, confirm a notification still fires. **(7)** Judgement call: after **Save token**, the update status you are looking at does not change on its own — you have to ask for a check. Is that surprising enough to be worth wiring, or is it fine? | UNTESTED. Automated: the chain order (a pasted token beats `gh`), that the download reaches for the injected source, that `DEFAULT_TOKEN_SOURCES` no longer carries a credential-store spelling that cannot read one, that the startup wiring exists at all (a **textual** test over `index.ts` — the defect was never in a module, every module behaved as written and the call site is in a file vitest cannot import; both mutations verified red), that a dev/test feed override never even **consults** a source, and that no token value can reach a log. **What no test can produce:** a real OS credential store with `gh` signed out — steps 1–5 are that machine, and nothing in CI has one. Step 6 is the blast radius of the hoist; step 7 is the only taste question. |
| **Clear and Compact buttons on the composer row (#903)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-24) — installable now. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: open a session and look at the row under the prompt box, beside the autonomy and model chips. There should be **Compact** and **Clear**. Press **Compact** — the session should compact, exactly as the ⋯ menu's *Compact conversation* does. **(2)** Press **Clear**. It must **ask first**, in the row itself: *Clear conversation?* with **Clear** and **Cancel**. Press **Cancel** — nothing is cleared. Press **Clear** again, then confirm: the conversation goes and the **"Conversation cleared — context starts fresh"** divider appears. **(3)** THE MENU STAYS: open the card's **⋯** menu. *Clear conversation* and *Compact conversation* must both still be there and still work. **(4)** WIDTH, and this is the one I most want your eye on: put three or four sessions side by side and make the window small. The row should **wrap onto a second line** rather than pushing anything off the end. Does the wrap look deliberate or does it look broken? ⚠️ Worth knowing before you try: the row was **measured** and it is not close to overflowing — a card in a two-way split at the smallest window the app allows has 219px of room for 157px of content — so you may have to work at it. **(5)** MIS-HIT RISK, the judgement call: Clear sits at the end of the row, after Compact, so it is not next to the model chip and not under the Send button. Is that far enough away, given it is the destructive one? And once the row wraps, that ordering no longer holds — does it still feel safe? **(6)** LOCKED STATES: on a session that is still starting, and on one that has ended, both buttons should be greyed **with a tooltip saying which** (hover them — a tooltip on a greyed button is the thing most likely to be broken, and it is the reason the tooltip sits on a wrapper rather than the button). **(7)** KEYBOARD, and please do this one properly: Tab to **Clear** and press Enter. Focus must land on **Cancel** inside the question — *not* vanish to the top of the card. Press Escape: the question goes and focus returns to **Clear**. Do it again and confirm with Enter. **(8)** POPPED OUT: pop a card into its own window (⤢) and do (7) there — the question and the focus must stay in that window. **(9)** DOUBLE-CLICK **Compact**: it should go grey for a moment and compact once, not twice. | UNTESTED. Automated: 11 unit tests on the shared action module (the lock table including the cleanly-exited case, the reason wording, and that both commands still type the literal `/clear` and `/compact` and still fall back to the PTY on a Terminal session) and 15 component tests through the real panel contribution (both buttons named in full, Compact sends immediately and refuses a second click while one is on the wire, Clear asks first and the first click sends nothing, focus goes into the question and back out, Escape from wherever focus actually is, the confirm is named differently from the trigger, and every lock state) — the prop-threading mutation was verified red, taking 9 tests with it. Three e2e: Clear from the row wiping a real conversation end to end on the default transport; the question and the focus landing in a **popped-out window's own document** (done-when 6, so it is not a human-only check any more); and the row measured **by summing its children** (#885's rule) on the narrowest card the suite can build. ⚠️ **An earlier width guard was thrown away for being vacuous** — it passed with the wrapping deleted — which is how the 219-vs-157 measurement in step 4 came to be recorded. **What no test judges:** steps 4, 5 and 6 — whether the wrap looks deliberate, whether Clear is far enough from the things you click often, and whether the greyed tooltips actually appear on your machine. |
| **A card stops wearing the name of a conversation it could not reopen (#886)** | **IN v0.8.100** (merged 2026-09-23) — installable now. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: open a session, send a prompt like `this history is about to be deleted`, and watch the card header take those words as its label. Quit switchboard, delete (or move) `%USERPROFILE%\.claude\projects`, and relaunch. The card must come back showing **+ task label** — *not* the old words. Before this fix it kept them for ever. **(2)** Send a new prompt in that card. It should now take the new prompt's words as its name; that is the half that was impossible before. **(3)** Check the **sessions rail** and the **card header** agree — both blank, then both showing the new name. They disagreeing was a real bug found in review. **(4)** THE ONE THAT MUST NOT HAVE BROKEN: type your own label on a card (click **+ task label**, type, Enter), then do the same delete-and-relaunch. **Your words must survive.** A label you typed is yours unconditionally, and nothing here is allowed to touch it. **(5)** The ordinary case: with `~/.claude/projects` left alone, quit and relaunch. Cards that resume should keep their labels exactly as before — this must not be clearing labels on every restart. | UNTESTED. Automated: seven unit tests (the label is dropped, the create reply does not hand it back, the next prompt names the card, a user label survives, a successful resume keeps its label, a card with no conversation is untouched, and an adopted conversation clears too) plus the e2e that deletes `~/.claude/projects` between two real app launches and asserts both the workspace file and the screen. All mutation-verified red. **What no test judges:** step 3's feel, and whether "+ task label" reading blank after a relaunch is surprising rather than correct. |
| **Report a problem carries the performance numbers (#927)** | ✅ **RELEASED in v0.8.99** (2026-09-23). Do it right after the #923 row, since it is how you send what #923 records. **(1)** With detailed capture **OFF** (the default), do **Help ▸ Report a problem**, choose *Just build the zip*, and open the zip. It should contain `performance-summary.txt`, and that file should carry the typing/switching numbers and say **"Detailed capture was OFF"**. The point: a report is useful even when you never touched the switch. **(2)** Turn capture ON, use the app a few minutes, report again. The summary should now say **"Detailed capture was ON"** with a keystroke count, and `performance-capture.jsonl` should be in the zip too. **(3)** File one to **GitHub** and read the issue in your browser. There should be a **Responsiveness** section in the body itself with the numbers in it — *you should not need to open the zip to see them*. That is the whole point of this item. **(4)** THE PRIVACY CHECK, again and more important here because this one is PUBLIC: read that issue body and confirm there is no prompt text, no file name, no project name and no session content anywhere in the Responsiveness section. **(5)** Take a zip to the desktop and open `performance-summary.txt` there. It should be readable on its own, with no app. | UNTESTED. Automated: the text renderer (percentiles, the off-vs-empty distinction, the layout-read alarm); the sanitizer that rebuilds the summary at the IPC boundary so an extra property cannot reach a public issue; that the section sits above the bundle section and below the user's own words; that a report with no numbers still files and says so; and a walk over every word of the rendered text against an allowlist. **What no test judges:** whether the section reads well in a real GitHub issue, and step 5. |
| **The performance instrument — summary command + detailed capture switch (#923)** | ✅ **RELEASED in v0.8.99** (2026-09-23). **This is P2-E21-02's tooling, and the whole epic is waiting on it, so it is the one to do first once it ships.** **On the LAPTOP.** **(1)** `Ctrl+Shift+P` → *Show performance summary*. With the app just launched it should say nothing has been measured yet rather than showing a table of zeros. Use the app for a minute, reopen it: rows appear for typing, switching session, opening Settings. **(2)** Read the sentences under the table. The background-delay line says "not measured yet" for the first minute after launch, then gives numbers. **(3)** THE SWITCH: `Ctrl+,` → **Diagnostics** → *Detailed performance capture*. It must be **off** on a machine that has never had it on. Turn it on. Quit and relaunch — it must still be on. Turn it off; relaunch; still off. **(4)** With it on, type in a composer for a bit, then reopen the summary. It should now report keystrokes captured, the largest conversation sampled, and — THE ONE THAT MATTERS — whether layout was measured while you typed. **That line should say zero.** If it is amber and non-zero, stop and tell me: that is PR #739's fix having regressed, and it is the single most valuable thing this feature can find. **(5)** **Settings → Diagnostics → Show the file.** It should open the folder with `performance-capture.jsonl` selected. The button is greyed until a capture exists. **(6)** **THE PRIVACY CHECK, and please actually do this one:** open that file in a text editor and search it for something you typed, a file name from one of your projects, and a phrase from a conversation. **None of the three may appear.** You should see numbers, the words `keystroke`/`session-switch`/`palette-open`, and your machine's details (version, OS, CPU, RAM). Nothing else. **(7)** THE ONE ONLY YOU CAN JUDGE: with capture ON, does typing feel any worse than with it off? On the desktop the difference was too small to measure; the laptop is the machine that decides. If it feels worse, say so — that would make the instrument unusable for its own purpose. **(8)** Then the actual job (P2-E21-02): leave it on, work a normal day with 3+ sessions, and send me the file. | UNTESTED. Automated: the percentile maths and the "no mean anywhere" rule; the capture file's switch, rotation, append-across-restarts and fail-open; **that the file contains no string we did not author** (a walk over every string in a real capture line, so a careless field added later goes red); the Settings section and its wiring; and the load-bearing one — **that with the switch off the instrumentation is genuinely absent**: the module is never imported, no listener is attached, the layout accessors are the browser's own objects by identity, and the composer's source imports nothing from the perf family. **Measured, not asserted:** `spike/findings/e21-923-instrument-cost.md` — on the dev desktop the capture's cost is below the noise floor (under 0.05 ms/keystroke), but that is the wrong machine, which is the entire reason this feature exists. **What no test can judge:** whether the summary screen actually answers "is it better?", and step 7. |
| **The missed-events digest — what quiet hours held while you were away (#483)** | ✅ **RELEASED in v0.8.99** (2026-09-23). **Setup:** command palette → *quiet hours*, set a window that covers the time you are testing; the dialog's bottom line should say it is on right now. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: click away to another app so switchboard is unfocused, then make a session need you (ask it to write a file, so it asks permission; or let one finish). You should get **no** sound, no pop-up, no spoken announcement. **(2)** Come back and open the Events drawer (Ctrl+E). The top should read **"1 notification was held while it was quiet"** and name that session, its state and the time. Check the name is the session's real name and the time is right. **(3)** Hold several, from two or more different sessions. The heading counts them and adds **"across N sessions"**. Past six, the extras should collapse to **"and N more"** rather than filling the drawer. **(4)** Press **Clear**. The summary goes. Reopen the drawer — still gone. Quit and relaunch — still gone. The events themselves should still be listed underneath as normal. **(5)** Open the drawer WITHOUT pressing Clear, close it, reopen it: the digest must still be there. Losing it by glancing in is the failure this is designed against. **(6)** Turn quiet hours off, let a session finish normally, open the drawer: there should be **no** digest at all. **(7)** Judgement calls: does the summary read well at the drawer's width, and is six named rows the right number before it collapses? **(8)** If you can leave it running across midnight, older rows should show a date, not just a bare clock time. | UNTESTED. Automated: the ordering, counts, overflow, session tally and earlier-day marking (unit); the notice's rendering, its accessible name and that it is absent when nothing was held; an e2e carrying the whole chain — hold an event inside a simulated quiet window, see the digest on return, Clear it, and assert the workspace file's held list is empty. Eleven mutations verified red. **What no test judges:** whether the summary reads well, whether six is the right cut, and the real overnight case. **Scope to know:** it digests what quiet hours SILENCED. Events that happen while the app is merely unfocused are not silenced (that is when pop-ups do fire) and already appear as ordinary Events rows, so they are deliberately not repeated here. |
| **Answer permissions and read questions from the Events drawer; All · Needed · By session filters (#420)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.98** (2026-09-22). **(1)** Open two sessions and get both asking permission (ask each to write a file). Open the Events drawer (Ctrl+E). Each entry shows the tool and the file, plus **Allow · Allow all · Deny**. Press **Deny** on one: that session carries on without it, and the session you were working in does NOT change. The other entry's buttons are still there. **(2)** Press **Allow** on the other: the file appears, and the entry drops out. **(3)** In one session, ask for two files in one go, so the entry says **+1 more**. Press **Allow all**: both get written, and the next thing that session asks for runs with no prompt at all. **(4)** Ask a session something that makes Claude ask you a multiple-choice question. The entry says **1 question waiting**. Click it to see the question and its choices, then **Answer in session**, which opens the session's own question panel. **(5)** Try **Needed** (seen entries disappear) and **By session** (entries in your Sessions-list order). Close and reopen the drawer: the filter you picked is still on. **(6)** Judgement call: does a row with three buttons read clearly at the drawer's width, or is it cramped? | Automated: unit tests for the filter and join rules and the panel's rendering (a click anywhere in the answer area never opens the card). The e2e runs on the stream fake: deny one session while the other stays held, allow runs the tool (checked on disk), Allow all answers both held calls and the follow-up (mutation-verified red), and the question list plus filters. What no test judges: how it looks and feels, and real `claude` question prompts |
| **Sessions shut down cleanly, and the log counts leftover programs (#719)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.98** (2026-09-22). **On the LAPTOP**, which is the machine this is for. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: use switchboard normally for a day, with your work projects and their Azure DevOps helper. Then open `%APPDATA%\switchboard\logs\switchboard.log` and search for `cpu heartbeat`. Each line now carries `sysProcs` (programs on the whole machine), `sysTop` (the most common ones by name, e.g. `"node.exe": 12`) and `children` (switchboard's own). **Watch whether `node.exe` in `sysTop` climbs over the day.** If it does, note roughly when (during sessions, or after closing cards / Restart), and send the log. **(2)** Close a session card, and Restart another. Each should go away as quickly as before, with no lag. **(3)** Quit switchboard with a session mid-answer. It may take up to about 3 s longer to disappear than before, and only then; it should never hang. **(4)** After you quit, `Get-Process node` in PowerShell should not show a batch of helpers left behind from switchboard's sessions. | UNTESTED. **Not a proven fix:** probing the real CLI showed the old close didn't normally leak helpers, and the one leak reproduced is inside the CLI (it abandons a slow-starting helper mid-session). This ships a cleaner shutdown plus the counts that will say where the laptop's 53 came from. Automated: graceful close / tree-kill backstop / quit drain, all mutation-verified; quit verified in the real app against 33 live Direct sessions |
| **Settings… in the File menu (#908)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.97** (2026-09-21). **(1)** Open the **File** menu: it reads **Open File…**, **Settings…** (with Ctrl+, shown beside it), a line, then **Exit**. Click **Settings…** and the Settings window opens. **(2)** Click into a session's prompt box, type a few words, then choose **File ▸ Settings…**: Settings still opens, and your words are still there after you close it. **(3)** Pop a session out (⤢), and from the popout window choose **File ▸ Settings…**. The main window should come forward with Settings open over it. **(4)** Ctrl+O and Ctrl+, still do what they did before. | Automated: the menu order, the shown-not-claimed shortcut and the click reaching the action (unit); an e2e that the menu click opens Settings with the composer focused, proven red by mutation. What a machine can't check: the popout case and how the real menu looks. Settings is ignored if another dialog is already open, same as Open File… |
| **The open tab is shaded in the session's colour; the name leaves the header (#905)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.96** (2026-09-21). **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: dock three sessions as tabs in one group. The open tab and the bar under it should be one shaded piece in that session's colour, with the tab name in bold. The other two tabs should sit flat on the strip in lighter text. Click through them: the shade follows, and the tabs don't shift sideways. **(2)** The header shows the badge and the task label (or a dashed **+ task label** box), with no session name. Click **+ task label** and type one. **(3)** Try all four themes (Ctrl+, → theme). Is every word on the shaded bar easy to read, especially **context**, the status pill and the label? **(4)** SPLIT: put two sessions side by side. Both open tabs are shaded; only the one in the group you last clicked is bold, and that group has the blue frame. **(5)** Pop a session out (⤢). Its window's tab and header are shaded the same way. **(6)** Double-click an empty part of the header: it still maximizes, and double-clicking again puts the layout back. **(7)** Is 24% shade the right strength? It's one number to change. | Automated: `--text` on the shade clears AA in 4 themes × 8 colours (unit); e2e measures the painted tab = header colour, every word on both AA in all four themes (label and prompt states), bold only in the focused group, and tab widths stable on switch. Each e2e half was proven red by mutation. What a machine can't judge: whether it *looks* right, and the strength |
| **Updates: notes for every release you missed, and an "Installing" window (#899)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.95** (2026-09-21), and **only shows on the update AFTER it**: install 0.8.95 the usual way (that install is still silent, and its notes are 0.8.95 only), then when 0.8.96 is offered: **(1)** if you are more than one release behind, the update box lists every missed version under its own heading, newest first; one release behind looks as before. **(2)** Press **Update**: the app closes and a small **"Installing, please wait…"** window appears (note which icon it shows), with no wizard and no "switchboard is running, click OK" box. **(3)** The app reopens by itself, and the Events drawer says **"You're now on v0.8.96"**. **(4)** Your desktop and Start-menu shortcuts are unchanged. | Unit tests pin the notes (order, eligibility, cap) and the installer arguments; mutation 7/7. **The installer window itself cannot be tested here**: running it would replace the live install. That needs your machine |
| **The Settings window, and a title bar that finally fits (#885 / #879)** | **In v0.8.93.** **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: press **Ctrl+,**. A **Settings** window opens with three sections — Appearance, Attention, Advanced. Change the **theme** in it and confirm the app repaints instantly, with the window still open. There is no Save button; that is deliberate. Press **Escape** to close. **(2)** THE BAR: look at the title bar. The five theme buttons, the two language buttons and **⑂ fork sessions** should be GONE. **🔔 notifications**, **🏷 labels**, **🔊 sounds**, **🗣 announce** and **🔓 auto-trust** must all still be there — those are the ones you need to kill mid-screen-share, and burying one would be a bug this item caused. **(3)** #879, THE REASON: make the window narrow — about 1024px wide, or just drag it in until it is roughly half a 1080p screen. Nothing in the title bar should fall off the right-hand edge, and the page must not scroll sideways. This used to be ~11 controls off screen. **(4)** MUSCLE MEMORY: press **Ctrl+Shift+P** and type *quiet hours*. It should open Settings scrolled to the Attention section, not a dialog of its own. Same for *phone push* and *task label size*. **(5)** ABOUT GOT SHORTER: click the version stamp at the top left. It should carry ONE **Settings…** button where it used to have three rows, plus **Check for updates…** (that one stayed on purpose). **(6)** THE ONE ONLY YOU CAN JUDGE: is a single scrolling window the right shape, or does it want a left-hand nav once there are more settings? It is ~15 preferences today. | Automated: the modal contract (Escape / click-away / focus), every absorbed control still writing through, all four themes still clearing AA, the RTL path, and an e2e guard that the bar fits at 1024px with no sideways scroll. What a machine cannot judge: whether the SHAPE is right, and whether anything you reach for daily is now too far away. |
| **Claude writes the task label, and keeps it up to date (#758)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.92 — installable now.** ⚠️ **THIS ONE SPENDS YOUR SUBSCRIPTION** (it shipped OFF in v0.8.92; #883 turns it on by default from 0.8.93). **(1)** THE SWITCH, which is now a THREE-STATE chip rather than a second one: with it reading **🏷 auto labels**, work a session for a while and confirm its label never changes by itself. Click the chip once — it should read **✨ AI labels**. (A second click reads **🏷 labels off**, a third returns to the start; the cycle exists because the title bar had no room for another chip, #879.) **(2)** THE MAIN ONE: give a session some real work and let it finish a turn. Within a few seconds its task label should become a short phrase describing **what it is actually doing** — not the folder name, and not the conversation's opening title. That is the whole feature and only you can say whether the phrase is any good. **(3)** THE POINT OF THE ITEM — DRIFT: keep working in that session until the job genuinely changes (finish one thing, start another). The label must **follow**, not stay stuck on the first job. This is the ask; if it never updates, the feature has failed even if step 2 looked fine. **(4)** IT DOESN'T PESTER: leave a session idle for half an hour with the switch on. Nothing should happen at all — no label change, no cost. Then send one trivial prompt ("say ok") and confirm it does **not** relabel for that either. **(5)** YOUR WORDS WIN: while a session is finishing a turn, type your own label into the card header. Your label must stay, permanently, and must never be overwritten. **(6)** OFF MEANS OFF: click the chip on to **🏷 labels off**. The last label it wrote stays on the card (that is deliberate), but nothing new is ever written, and every auto label leaves the cards and the rail. Clear the box by hand if you want a label gone for good. **(7)** AND BACK: click once more to return to **🏷 auto labels** — the labels you had should reappear instantly, because hiding never deleted them. **(8)** COST SANITY, the one worth doing properly: with it on across a normal working day, check `/cost` or your usage and confirm the labeler is a rounding error rather than something you can feel. If it is noticeable, say so — the cadence numbers are a judgement call I made and they are one constant each to change. | ⚠️ **UNTESTED.** Automated: the containment was **measured against the real CLI, not assumed** (`spike/probes/758/`, findings in `spike/findings/758-label-containment.md`, claude 2.1.272) — and the measurement changed the design. **`--restricted` ALONE leaves 34 tools and your MCP server, keeping Read/Glob/Grep and SendMessage/ListAgents** — the exact surface #760 used to read transcripts and message six sessions across four unrelated projects. Only `--tools ""` empties it and only `--strict-mcp-config` evicts the servers, so the labeler runs with **nothing**: 0 tools, 0 servers, proven against a 33-tool control. Plus 41 unit tests on the decision rules (when to spend, the ordering of the gates, the last-writer discard) and the runner (the prompt is never an argument, every failure resolves, a timeout kills the process tree). **What no test can judge: whether the labels are any good, and whether the drift in step 3 actually tracks the work** — which is the entire feature. ⚠️ Also worth knowing: the probe caught the model **refusing** a prompt that read like an injection, so the label prompt's wording is load-bearing; if labels ever silently stop updating, that is the first thing to suspect. |
| **The card names itself the instant you prompt it, and AI labels are on by default (#883)** | **In v0.8.93.** Owner feedback on #758 after using v0.8.92. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: open a brand-new session and send your first prompt. The card's task label should become **your own words** immediately — no waiting, no cost. **(2)** THEN IT IMPROVES: let that turn finish. Within ~15 s Claude's own label should REPLACE the one from your prompt, and it should read better than your raw prompt did. **(3)** A SLASH COMMAND: start a session and send `/clear` (or any command). The label must read the command as a person writes it — **not** raw `<command-name>` markup, which is the bug the history picker shipped once. **(4)** ON BY DEFAULT: on a machine that has never had this setting, the chip should already read **✨ AI labels**. Clicking once gives **🏷 auto labels**, again gives **🏷 labels off**, again returns to the start. **(5)** IT ONLY FILLS A BLANK: on a session that already has a label, send another prompt — the label must NOT be replaced by your new prompt text. **(6)** YOUR WORDS STILL WIN: type your own label, then send a prompt — it must survive untouched. **(7)** A MENTION: send a prompt that starts with an `@mention` of another session. The label should stay blank until Claude's label arrives, rather than reading "Context from "…" (session)other-session" — refusing is deliberate. | ⚠️ **UNTESTED.** Automated: 32 tests on the label decisions (including the instant label's command handling, its refusal on an injected-context turn, and that it never replaces an existing label) and 256 on the IPC wiring, plus the store's default flip asserted in both directions. **What no test can judge: whether the instant label is actually useful for the second or two before Claude's replaces it**, and whether the default-on setting feels right rather than presumptuous — it reverses the opt-in the ticket originally specified, on the owner's own instruction. |
| **Task labels get three lines, and a size setting (#877)** | **In v0.8.93.** Owner feedback on #758: "at least three lines max", then "default to the full width and fill the space, but have options to make it smaller". **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: with **✨ AI labels** on, let a session earn a real label. It should now be a fuller phrase and wrap onto **up to three lines** — on the card header AND in the sessions list. A long label must be clamped at three with an `…`, not run on for ever. **(2)** THE ROW LAYOUT, which is the other half of the ask: in the sessions list, a session's state (*working*, *needs you*, *idle*) now sits to the RIGHT OF ITS NAME rather than on the second line. Drive a session until it needs permission and confirm **both** the state and the task label are visible at once — before this, a session that needed you lost its label entirely. **(3)** THE SETTING: `Ctrl+Shift+P` → *task label size*, or the button in the **About** panel. Pick **Compact** — every label everywhere should drop to one line **immediately**, on cards that are already open, without a restart. **Medium** gives two, **Full** three. **(4)** IT STICKS: quit and relaunch — the size you picked must still be in force, and the dialog must show it selected. **(5)** NOTHING IS LOST: on Compact, click a clamped label to edit it — the whole label must be in the box, not the truncated version. **(6)** THE JUDGEMENT CALL, and the only part no test can make: whether three lines is actually the right amount of room, and whether the fuller phrases Claude now writes are better than the six-word ones or just longer. If the rail feels too tall at Full, say so — the default is one constant to change. | ⚠️ **UNTESTED.** Automated: 7 tests on the shared size vocabulary, 9 on the dialog (every size offered, the stored one checked, the pick written through with no Save button, Escape and click-away write nothing), 7 on the rail row (the clamp comes from the SETTING and not a constant; the em dash holds the space open before a label arrives; **the label reaches a screen reader even on a row that needs you** — the regression this item fixed, and one invisible from the pixels), plus store, session-store and IPC tests that the size survives a reload and that a value outside the vocabulary cannot be written. All six load-bearing fixes were mutation-checked — reverted one at a time, each killing exactly its own test. **What no test can judge: whether the labels read better with the room, which is the entire point.** |
| **The Terminal tab and the ⋯-menu Terminal/Direct switch are gone (#873)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.92 — installable now.** **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: open any session. The card must have **three** tabs — **Session**, **Changes**, **History** — and no Terminal tab at all. **(2)** THE MENU: open a session's **⋯** menu. There must be no *Transport: … — switch to …* entry and no *Restart session now*. Everything else in that menu must still work — Clear conversation, Notify when done, Fork, Sound, the autonomy rows. **(3)** THE KEY: press ``Ctrl+` `` on a focused session — nothing should happen, and no card should change tabs. Open the palette (`Ctrl+Shift+P`) and type *terminal*: there should be no **Toggle Terminal view** command. **(4)** THE MIGRATION, and the one that matters if you ever set a session to Terminal mode: that card must come back on Direct and keep working — same card, same name, same conversation, nothing lost. Then check `%APPDATA%\switchboard\logs\switchboard.log` for a line about sessions moved off the **removed Terminal transport**, with a count. If you have no such card there will be no line, which is correct. **(5)** THE TRUST CHIP: hover **🔓 auto-trust / 🔒 ask trust** in the title bar. It stays greyed, and the tooltip must now explain that **nothing can ask** and that your choice is kept — it must NOT tell you to switch a session to Terminal from its ⋯ menu, which is the instruction this item exists to remove. **(6)** FIND: `Ctrl+F` in a session should show a single position (`3 of 14`) with **no** *Terminal (scrollback only)* group beside it and no *open the Terminal tab* notice. **(7)** NOTHING ELSE MOVED: send a prompt, answer an approval, open **Changes**, pop a card out and dock it back, hide a card and bring it back — all exactly as before. | ⚠️ **UNTESTED.** Automated: the workspace **v1→v2 migration** is proven by unit tests (a stored `pty` choice is CLEARED so the card follows the Direct default *and* still honours a `SWITCHBOARD_TRANSPORT` override; an explicit `stream` choice is left alone; a card that never chose is untouched; the log line fires only when something actually moved), plus the panel registry (three panels, nothing kept mounted), the retired command, the unregistered Terminal find provider, and the reworded trust and transcript-fallback copy. **What no test can answer: whether anything you relied on the Terminal tab for is now unreachable.** The PTY transport itself is untouched and still works — only the way to LOOK at one was removed — so if you find yourself needing it, say so, because that is the signal this removal was wrong. ⚠️ Also note what the automated suite LOST with the tab: the end-to-end proofs of input→pty→render, of the CLI keeping every key inside a terminal, and of grouped find over two surfaces. Those are recorded in PROGRESS.md and in the specs themselves. |
| **The main window comes back to the monitor it was on after the displays sleep (#864)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.92 — installable now.** **This one needs your real rig, and the monitor must be POWERED OFF (or genuinely asleep), not locked or blanked** — a screensaver does not reproduce it, because the bug is the display actually leaving the bus. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: put the switchboard window on a SECONDARY monitor, leave it a moment, power that monitor off, then power it back on. The window must return to that monitor at the same size and position. Today it reappears on your primary. **(2)** THE PERSISTENT VARIANT, which is the half that made it permanent: with the window on the secondary, power the monitor off, then QUIT switchboard while it is still off. Power the monitor back on and relaunch — it must open on the secondary, not the primary. **(3)** MAXIMIZED: repeat (1) with the window maximized on the secondary. It must come back maximized **on that monitor**. **(4)** NOTHING ELSE CHANGED: with a session popped out onto the secondary, repeat (1). The popout keeps its old behaviour — it does **not** jump back on its own, Events still offers you the restore. Only the main window returns by itself, and that difference is deliberate. **(5)** A DISPLAY IT HAS NEVER SEEN: plug in something new — a projector, a TV, a monitor in a new position. The main window must stay exactly where it is. It must never be pulled onto a display it has not lived on before. **(6)** THE COMMUTE: work at the desk, undock to the laptop alone, work there, dock again. Each arrangement should remember its own position. | ⚠️ **UNTESTED, and the automated tests genuinely cannot reach this one** — CI runners have a single display and no way to detach it, so no amount of green CI is evidence here. Automated: 19 unit tests on the decision logic (it restores only to an arrangement the window has already lived on; never onto bounds no current display can show; never moves a window already in place; carries the maximized state back; evicts the oldest arrangement past the cap) plus store tests proving the damaging half — that a save made while a monitor is asleep **cannot** overwrite the note for the arrangement that included it, that it survives quit/relaunch, and that a workspace file written before this change loads unchanged. **What no test can answer: whether it actually comes back on YOUR hardware.** The bug is a real DisplayPort detach and nothing here can produce one. |
| **Fork a session so a second one starts knowing everything the first does (#801)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.91 — installable now.** ⚠️ **EXPERIMENTAL and OFF by default**, so nothing below is reachable until you turn it on. **(1)** THE SWITCH: with it off, open any session's **⋯** menu and confirm there is **no** fork entry at all — absent, not greyed. Now turn on **⑂ fork sessions** in the title bar. **(2)** NOTHING TO FORK YET: open a brand-new session and check its **⋯** menu — the entry should still be absent, because it has no conversation yet. Send it a prompt, then look again: now it appears. **(3)** THE MAIN ONE: in a session with some real history (a few prompts, let it edit something), choose **Fork into a new session**. A new card opens in the same folder. **Ask the new session something only the original could know** — *"what did I ask you to do earlier?"* — it must answer from the inherited conversation. That is the whole feature and nothing else proves it. **(4)** THE ORIGINAL IS UNTOUCHED, which is the damaging failure if it is wrong: go back to the first session. It must be exactly as you left it — not restarted, not reset, nothing appended, and still usable. Send it a prompt and confirm it still works. **(5)** THEY DIVERGE: tell the fork a new fact, then ask the original about it. The original must know nothing. Then the reverse. **(6)** TWO CARDS, TWO CONVERSATIONS: confirm both cards keep working side by side and neither one's Session view starts showing the other's messages — if they ever converge on the same content, stop and report it. **(7)** THE SWITCH GOES BOTH WAYS: with a forked session open, turn **⑂ fork sessions** back off. The open fork must keep working normally; only the menu entry goes. **(8)** RESTART: quit and relaunch with the flag off, and confirm no session tries to fork itself on restore. | ⚠️ **UNTESTED.** Automated: the CLI contract was **measured, not assumed** — two committed probes drove the real `claude` 2.1.272 (`spike/probes/801/`, write-up `spike/findings/e11-801-fork-adoption.md`) and proved the fork carries the history, leaves the source transcript **byte-identical** (sha256, size and mtime), lands in the target folder's directory, and announces **our** session id rather than the parent's — the last one being what stops two cards tailing one transcript. Plus 28 unit tests: the capability gate (both fakes deliberately withhold it, so the path is unreachable for them), the argv and its refusal to spawn an unpinned fork, every IPC refusal (flag off, no capability, source not on disk, a dash-led or traversing id, a bad folder), and the one that matters most — that the card records the **fork's** conversation and never the source's. **What no test can answer: whether the forked session is actually useful** — whether it picks up the work as if it had been there, which is the entire point and only a real conversation can judge it. Also unmeasured: forking a conversation that is **live in another process** (every probe forked an idle one). |
| **#815 Report a problem — Help menu, with a GitHub issue or email destination** | Open **Help ▸ Report a problem…** (or the palette, `Ctrl+Shift+P` → *report*). Check **Send report** is dead until you type a subject. Try all three destinations: **Just build the zip** (a zip should appear in Explorer — open it and confirm it holds `logs/`, `workspace.json` and `bundle-info.txt`, and **no** transcripts); **Open an email** (your mail app opens, addressed, with the zip path in the body); **Create a GitHub issue** — on the laptop this should need NO setup if `gh` is signed in, and the issue should appear on the repo carrying your text plus version/system details. Confirm the token field is **not** offered when `gh` already works, and that the box always says the zip is not attached | UNTESTED. The GitHub path has never run against the real API from inside the app — only against a stubbed one. Also worth checking the report survives with no network: the zip should still be written and revealed |
| **#719 CPU heartbeat — the app records its own per-process CPU every minute** | On the LAPTOP: use switchboard normally for an hour or two, then open `%APPDATA%\switchboard\logs\switchboard.log` and search for `cpu heartbeat`. Expect one line a minute. Each names every process with a `cores` figure (`1.0` = one core fully busy, on any machine) and `lagMaxMs` (how long the app was unresponsive). A quiet minute legitimately lists no processes at all — only ones using a measurable share are named — so `procs: []` on an idle app is correct, not a broken reading. **If a freeze happens, do not clear the log**: the busy minutes are recorded at `warn` and name the responsible process | UNTESTED. Instrumentation only — it fixes nothing, and the real test is whether the NEXT freeze names its own burner. Verified end-to-end on the dev desktop only, which has never reproduced #719 |
| **Hand one session's context to another by dragging its chip (#799)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.91 — installable now.** Open **two** sessions in different folders and give the first one some real work to do (a few prompts, let it edit something) so it has a conversation worth handing over. **(1)** THE GESTURE: find the small **context** chip in the first session's header and drag it onto the *second* session's prompt box. A dialog should open — it must NOT inject anything on the drop itself. **(2)** THE NUMBERS, which are the point: all three options (**Last response**, **Summary handoff**, **Full excerpt**) show a rough token size, and they should differ substantially. **Summary handoff** must be pre-selected — it is the documented default. Sanity-check the sizes against the session: a long conversation should not report a 20-token handoff. **(3)** CHOOSE ONE: press **Add to prompt box**. The text appears in the second session's prompt box as a block headed *Context from "…" (session)<first session>* — and **nothing is sent**. **(4)** THE ENTER: type something of your own ("carry on from here") and press Enter. The other session should receive the context AND your line, and should be able to act on it — this is the real question: *is the handoff good enough that the second session can actually continue the work?* Only you can answer that. **(5)** CANCEL: drag another chip, press **Cancel** — nothing should appear anywhere. **(6)** SELF-DROP: drag a session's own chip onto its own prompt box; it should refuse with a note rather than doing nothing. **(7)** A THIN SESSION: open a brand-new session and drag its chip somewhere — options should read *nothing recorded* rather than looking full. **(8)** A LONG ONE: do it from a very long conversation (this repo) and confirm the dialog says it covers only the most recent part, and that the same caveat appears in what the other session receives. | Automated: the offer builder (sizes copied off the package's own section estimates, not recomputed — pinned with deliberately wrong estimates; coverage carried into every option; empty marked; control characters stripped), the dialog (default is Level 2, Escape/Cancel/click-away commit nothing, thin options marked), the composer (drop opens the dialog and injects nothing, self-drop refused, a broker refusal treated as no offer, the drop swallowed so it can't also open a session), and the seam (the parked block goes verbatim on the next Enter with no sibling header, and an Enter before it settles doesn't send it). **What no test can answer: whether the handoff is actually good enough to continue the work.** |
| **A session can fetch another session's handoff itself (#800)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.91 — installable now.** The agent-pulled twin of the context chip above: no dragging, the session asks. Open **two** sessions in different folders; give the first one real work to do (a few prompts, let it edit something). **(1)** THE MAIN ONE, and the whole point: in the *second* session, say *"pick up where &lt;first session&gt; got to"* — in your own words, naming no tool. It should go and fetch the handoff on its own and then tell you what that session was doing. **The real question only you can answer: did it choose the right tool?** It has `get_session_output` (raw recent output) sitting next to `get_session_context` (the structured handoff), and reaching for the wrong one gets a worse answer rather than an error. **(2)** THE LEVELS: ask for *"just the last thing it said"* and then *"the whole handoff"* — the first should come back noticeably smaller. **(3)** A THIN SESSION: open a brand-new session that has done nothing and ask another session to pick up its work. It must be told plainly there is nothing recorded — and, if it asked for one of the smaller levels, that the fuller handoff may still have something. It must **not** be told a fuller level is pointless, and it must not conclude that session "did nothing" when the truth is there was nothing to read. **(4)** A LONG ONE: do it from a very long conversation (this repo) — what the asking session receives must say it covers only the most recent part. **(5)** IT ONLY READS: confirm nothing appears in anyone's prompt box and nothing is sent to the session being read. **(6)** BAD INPUT: ask it for a detail level that doesn't exist (*"give me the 'everything' level of X"*); it should be told the three real ones rather than quietly getting the default. | Automated: the vocabulary and its default (shared with the drop dialog, so a fourth level can't be offered by one and refused by the other), the unknown-level refusal, the level reaching the query core untouched (`['state','excerpt','everything',7,undefined]` pinned exactly), the coverage statement surviving verbatim, the fence, the empty-at-one-level wording, the in-flight bound, and a full end-to-end run through a real transcript on disk. **What no test can answer: whether an agent that was never told this tool exists actually finds it and picks it over `get_session_output`** — the descriptions are what do that, and only a real session can judge it. |
| **Sessions can leave each other notes on a shared board (#796)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.91 — installable now.** There is no UI at all for this: it is a thing sessions do, so every check is a plain-English request to a session. Open **two** sessions. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: in session A say *"pin the current build status on the shared board where the other sessions can find it"*, then in session B say *"check the board for a build status"*. B should find it and tell you what A wrote, including that A left it. **The question only you can answer: did A pick the right tool?** `send_to_session` sits right beside this one and promises to reach a *person* — an agent that wanted to notify you and pinned a note instead has left it somewhere nobody is looking, and nothing will report that. **(2)** DISCOVERY: in a third session (or B again) ask *"what's on the shared board?"* without naming a key. You should get a list of names with who left them and how big they are — and **not** the contents. **(3)** NOTHING IS DELIVERED: confirm that pinning a note in A causes **nothing** to appear in B's prompt box, no badge, no sound. B must have to go and look. **(4)** A MISSING NAME: ask B to read a name nobody has pinned. It should say so plainly and tell you what *is* there — it must not conclude the board is empty or that A did nothing. **(5)** AFTER A IS GONE: close session A, then ask B to read A's note again. The note must still be there, and B should be told its author is no longer running rather than trying to go and ask it. **(6)** IT DOES NOT SURVIVE A QUIT: restart the app and ask about the board — it should be empty. That is deliberate, not a bug. | Automated: every cap (key, value, key count, and the board's total — including the one accounting case that matters, an overwrite that SHRINKS a value not being refused by a full board), refusal-rather-than-truncation, the unknown-key answer that names real keys, attribution resolved from the token rather than the child's argv, the publisher-has-exited flag, list-without-values, insertion order, and a real A-publishes/B-reads round trip over two separate endpoints plus the same end to end through the bus child. **What no test can answer: whether an agent reaches for the board when it should, and — more importantly — whether it reaches for it when it actually wanted to tell YOU something.** |
| **Previous conversations are described by what you asked, not by markup (#846)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.91 — installable now.** Owner-reported while hand-testing #836 on v0.8.90. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: open the **🕘** picker in a folder you have real history in (this repo). **No row may contain angle-bracket markup** — no `<command-name>`, no `<command-message>`. Rows that previously read `<command-name>/clear</command-name>…` should now read the first real thing you asked in that conversation. Measured: 28 of the 40 newest conversations here open with a slash command, so there should be plenty of before/after to see. **(2)** COMMANDS-ONLY: find (or make) a conversation that was *nothing but* commands — start a session, run `/clear`, close it. Its row must read **`/clear`** — not blank, not markup. **(3)** WITH ARGUMENTS: in the picker, a conversation that opened with a command that took an argument should show it in full (`/next-item 818`), not the bare name. **(4)** THE FEED, which changed too: in a session, run a slash command that takes an argument. The collapsed block in the **Session** view should now be labelled with the argument included — before this it showed only the command name. **(5)** THE INVISIBLE HALF: have one session read another's work (`@`-mention it, or ask it to). The context it receives must not carry `<command-name>` markup as the other session's stated goal — this was the same bug reaching session hand-offs, where you would never have seen it. **(6)** NOTHING ELSE MOVED: an ordinary conversation that began with a real question must be described exactly as before. | Automated: the shared helper over the real measured shapes (including the multi-line blob the CLI actually writes, and the deliberate non-matches — a prompt that merely *mentions* `<command-name>` is still a prompt); the prompt reader skipping a command block; the commands-only fallback; a hand-off package refusing to take a command as its goal; and the full feed-blocks and FeedView families for the renderer change. What no machine can judge: whether the descriptions now actually help you recognise a conversation at a glance on a machine with real history — which is the judgement that found this bug in the first place. |
| **A long-held maximize no longer replays a days-old layout (#818)** | ✅ **Shipped in v0.8.91 — installable now.** **Your laptop is the test rig**: it has a real stale maximize (the chip reads `▦ Grid · maximized`, taken days ago on a background tab, 15-card snapshot). **(1)** THE MAIN ONE, and do it before anything else while the stale state still exists: find the maximized session, make sure it is **not** currently filling the workspace, and double-click its header. It must **maximize that session** — not fold your workspace back to an old arrangement. Before this fix that gesture restored the layout from the day the maximize was taken. **(2)** THE ORDINARY UNDO, unchanged: double-click a session's header to maximize it, then double-click again within a minute — the workspace must come back exactly as it was, including anything you had hidden by hand. This is the case the fix must not have cost anything. **(3)** THE PARTIAL RESTORE: maximize a session, then go and re-open a couple of the folded sessions from the Collapsed strip and leave them open. Now undo the maximize — the ones you re-opened must **stay** open, while the ones you left folded come back as they were. **(4)** NOT A TRAP, still: with a maximize held, click a folded session in the strip to go look at it — it must come back and stay; then undo the maximize and the arrangement still restores. **(5)** A SESSION CREATED SINCE: maximize, add a new session, then undo — the new one must be left exactly where it is, not swept into the old arrangement. **(6)** `Ctrl+Shift+M` must behave identically to the double-click in (1) and (2). | Automated: the standing check and the snapshot filter as pure unit tables (a card re-expanded since, one pushed further down, one still folded as the maximize left it, one the maximize was never allowed to fold, the maximized card judged against `expanded`, closed cards); the inherited-snapshot trap (a stale maximize must not hand its snapshot to the next one); and the existing e2e that reveals a folded card mid-maximize and still restores. What a machine cannot judge: whether (1) feels like the app doing the obvious thing on **your** actual stale state — that state exists on exactly one machine and cannot be reproduced in CI. |
| **Open a previous conversation from a session card (#836)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.90 (2026-09-16) — ready to hand-test.** **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: open a session in a folder you have used with Claude Code before (this repo is ideal — it has hundreds). Click the **🕘** in the card header. You should get a list of that folder's past conversations, newest first, each with a real description — Claude's own title, or your opening question in quotes — and when you last worked on it. Pick one: a **NEW** card opens on that conversation, and the card you clicked from must be completely untouched. Scroll the new card's Session view: the old conversation's history should be there. **(2)** SEARCH: type a few letters of a conversation you remember; the list narrows. Type nonsense; it says nothing matches. **(3)** ALL PROJECTS: click **This folder** to switch it to **All projects** — conversations from your other repos appear, each with its folder beside it. Pick one from a *different* folder: the new card must open in THAT folder, not this one. **(4)** NO DOUBLE-OPEN: with a conversation already open in a card, open the picker again — that row should be greyed out and say *already open*, and clicking it should do nothing. **(5)** `+ SESSION`: click **+ session** and choose a folder you have history in. The picker should appear by itself; **Start a new conversation instead** at the bottom, and `Esc`, must both give you a normal new session. Choose a folder with NO history and no picker should appear at all — straight to a new session, as before. **(6)** A POPPED-OUT CARD: pop a card into its own window and use its 🕘 there; the new card should land in that window. | Automated tests already cover the listing, the descriptions and their fallback, the refusals, the dialog, and one full end-to-end open-and-resume. What needs a human: whether the descriptions actually help you RECOGNISE a conversation on a real machine with real history, and whether the `+ session` interruption is welcome or annoying. ⚠️ **PARTLY ANSWERED, AND IT FAILED:** the owner ran exactly that check on v0.8.90 and found rows showing raw `<command-name>/clear</command-name>` markup — filed as **#846**, fixed for 0.8.91. Re-run the description half of this row together with #846's, not before it. |
| **`@Name` brings that session's recent work into your message (#798)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.90 (2026-09-16) — ready to hand-test.** Needs two or three sessions in different folders, and the session you mention must have said something first. **(1)** THE MAIN ONE: in session A, ask it something so it has a few replies. In B's message box type ``take @A's last output and summarise it`` and press **Enter**. Your turn in B should show A's recent conversation **in front of** your words, headed *Recent output from A*, inside a marked block — and your `@A` should read `"A" (session)`, not `@A`. **(2)** NOTHING IS EATEN: send `mail me at dan@example.com about @nosuchsession please` — it must arrive exactly as typed, with nothing attached. **(3)** SAME NAME TWICE: open one repo twice so two sessions share a name, then mention it. **Nothing should send.** A note under the box names both candidates *with their folders*, and your draft stays put. Now mention it by the session id from that note — that should send, with context. **(4)** YOURSELF: in B type `note to @B` and send — your own session is never resolved, so it goes as typed. **(5)** KEEP TYPING: type `ask @A about the cache`, press **Enter**, and immediately keep typing a few more words. The extra words must still be in the box afterwards. **(6)** TERMINAL MODE: repeat (1) in a session running in Terminal rather than Direct — the context arrives as one pasted block. **(7)** TIMING, EXPECTED NOT A BUG: mention a session the instant its reply appears and you may get "has not produced any readable output yet" — its conversation has not reached disk yet. A second later it works. | Automated: the finder and prompt builder as pure tables (longest name wins even when the shorter one also ends legally, names with spaces and apostrophes including the typographic one, code fences and inline code, email addresses, `@` at end, case, the typed-vs-list spelling, no second mention inside a matched one, the total size cap and its boundary); the resolver against a **real** `SessionQueries` and real transcripts (attribution, #764's wording, the fence, no-transcript, own session, renamed-away, ids, two sessions differing only by case, ambiguity); the IPC handler (argument checks never reach the query core, a throw becomes `null`, the capability, the preload's channel); the composer's send path (refusal keeps the draft, failed lookup fails open, slash commands and forwarded messages never resolved, the send guard released, held sibling messages forwarded once, text typed during the lookup kept, a trimmed trailing space still clears the box); and an e2e driving two real Direct sessions end to end. Two mutation rounds, 46 mutants — every survivor killed except three equivalents, documented. What a machine cannot judge: whether the injected block reads as *context* rather than as something you said, at a glance and in all four themes; and (6), which has no automated coverage at all |
| **Mention another session by typing `@` in the message box (#797)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.90 (2026-09-16) — ready to hand-test.** Open two or three sessions in different folders first. **(1)** In one session's message box, type `@`: the others appear with their colours, and **the session you're typing in is not listed**. **(2)** Type a few letters of a name: the list narrows. Press **Tab**: `@ThatSession ` goes into the box with the cursor after it, and nothing is sent. **(3)** Mid-sentence: type `please look at @`, then pick a session. The words before the `@` are still there. **(4)** Type a session's **whole** name after `@` and press **Enter**: the message sends straight away rather than "completing" to itself. **(5)** THE SUBSTRING RULE: with a session named, say, `TradingApp`, type `ping @app` and press **Enter**. It sends `ping @app` exactly as typed and must NOT turn into `@TradingApp`. Then retype, press **↓** first, then **Enter**: now it completes to `@TradingApp`. **(6)** **Esc** closes the list; typing more of the same word keeps it closed, and a new `@` further along opens it again. **(7)** Click into the middle of an `@name` you already wrote and press **Enter**: it just sends, and nothing gets replaced. **(8)** An email address (`dan@example.com`) never opens a list and is sent unchanged. **(9)** A session whose process has ended is still listed, marked **exited**. **(10)** The `/` command list still behaves as before, in all four themes | Automated: the pure trigger and list rules (every boundary and name-ending character, the caret must be at the END of the word, prefix-first ranking, own session out, exited kept, the Enter action table), the channel's handler and read-only capability, `accentColor` present/absent, and component tests through the real composer (list, one fetch per opening, colour dot and fallback, Tab caret position, full-name Enter sends, substring Enter sends the literal, moved-to-row and hovered-row complete, sticky Esc and its forgetting, caret inside a mention, Enter/Tab held while the list loads, refused and rejected reads, no stale list on reopen, highlight reset, `/(@x` precedence). Two mutation rounds, every survivor now killed. Since #798 the mention also brings that session's recent work with it at send — test the two together. What a machine cannot judge: whether the popup's colour dot and **exited** tag read clearly at 10–11px in all four themes |
| **Token counts on the card stop counting each reply several times (#807)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.90 (2026-09-16) — ready to hand-test.** **(1)** THE BIG ONE: open a session that has been running a while, ideally one that used a helper agent. `↑`, `↓` and `⛁` on the card header should be noticeably **smaller** than you remember, often by half or more. The `~$` estimate built from them shrinks by the same factor. **(2)** In that session's **Terminal** tab type `/exit`. The `~` estimate becomes Claude Code's own `$` figure. The two should now be close; before this fix the estimate could be several times higher. **(3)** Ask a session to start a helper agent ("use a subagent to count the files here"). While it works, `↓` should climb steadily and never jump by thousands per step. **(4)** Restart switchboard with a card open. After that card's next reply its figures should be at the corrected size, not the old inflated one. **(5)** A session that never uses a helper agent: its tokens-in and cache figures shrink too (every reply was counted once per content block), but less dramatically | Automated: a real (sanitized) helper-agent transcript with growing copies, which failed on the old code with exactly the inflated totals; the reset on rebind; hostile values; the thinking breakdown replacing with its copy; two mutation rounds (14/15, then 13/14 — the one survivor is equivalent on every measured transcript). Measured against Claude Code's own ledger over 25 sessions: input/cache 0.98–1.013 (21 exact), output 0.97–1.00. ⚠️ **Not measured: a RESUMED conversation.** If a resumed card reads well above Claude Code's `/exit` figure, report it. That's the one case the corpus couldn't check. What a machine cannot judge: whether the smaller numbers match what `/cost` tells you inside the session |
| **The usage readout shows how much output was thinking (#789)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.90 (2026-09-16) — ready to hand-test.** **(1)** In an **Opus or Fable** session, ask something that needs reasoning. The card header's tokens-out figure should become `↓ <n> (<m> thinking)`, the parenthesised part fainter, `<m>` smaller than `<n>`. **(2)** Hover the thinking part: a percentage, and "already counted in the output figure". Hover `↓` itself: still "Output tokens". **(3)** Keep working: both climb together; the thinking figure is NEVER larger than the output one. **(4)** A Haiku session or a trivial no-reasoning prompt shows no `(… thinking)` at all. **(5)** A narrow card, all four themes: the extra text must not shove the branch or cost off the header, and the grey must be legible at 10px. **(6)** Restart switchboard: a card that showed a thinking figure shows it again. | Automated: per-line reader (11 hostile-JSON cases incl. the real `null` the CLI writes on `<synthetic>` lines), watcher accumulation (output stays 10, not 17), render guard + 1–99% clamping, estimate unchanged, strip text/tooltip against real `en.json`, drift detector reporting a rename or sibling of `thinking_tokens`, persistence on both write paths, and a 23-mutant round (23/23). What a machine cannot judge: whether the header still fits in a narrow card, and whether the faint grey reads. |
| **The Feed names which subagent is talking (#788)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.90 (2026-09-16) — ready to hand-test.** **(1)** In a **Terminal mode** session, ask Claude to do something that fans out to several agents at once ("use three parallel agents to …"). In the Session tab you should see their work indented behind a dashed line, and each stretch should open with a small grey caption like `Subagent · Explore`. **(2)** THE POINT OF THE ITEM: with two or more running at once their replies arrive mixed together, and a NEW caption must appear every time the speaker changes. If you see one long indented run with a single caption at the top, the grouping did not take. **(3)** If two of the helpers have the SAME name — e.g. two `deep-research-specialist`s — each caption should gain a short code (`· a3f9c1`) so you can tell them apart. Only then; a single helper of that name must NOT show a code. **(4)** The caption must line up exactly with the dashed line of the blocks under it — no half-pixel step. **(5)** Switch the detail chip to **quiet**: the helper work and its captions should vanish together. Back to **normal**: both return. **(6)** Check that "NEW PROMPT" no longer rules off a helper's *task* prompt — it should appear above YOUR prompts only. **(7)** A session that never spawns a helper must look exactly as it did before: no captions, nothing new. **(8)** A **Direct mode** session shows no helper work at all — that is expected, not a bug. | Automated: 51 unit/component tests + a 23-mutant round (23/23 killed) + an e2e case that writes two real interleaved subagent files and asserts three captions. What a machine cannot judge: whether the caption reads as a margin note rather than a heading at a dozen per turn, and whether the grey is legible in all four themes at 10px. |
| **Cost on the card: the rate table is corrected, and Claude Code's own figure supersedes the estimate at the end (#787)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.90 (2026-09-16) — ready to hand-test.** **(1)** Open any session and work in it until the header shows a cost. It reads `~$x.xx` with a tilde — that is our estimate. Hover: it must say it is an estimate, a floor, and that you are billed by subscription. **(2)** THE BIG ONE, and the reason this row exists: **the number itself should now be roughly 2.4× SMALLER than you remember for an Opus session.** The old table priced Opus at $15/$75 when it is $5/$25. If a long session still reads like it cost $200, the fix did not take. A Fable session moves the other way — about 2.6× *larger*. **(3)** THE EXACT FIGURE, and it only arrives one way: open the session's **Terminal** tab and type `/exit`. The tilde should disappear and the number should change to Claude Code's own — hover says so. **Restarting or closing the session from switchboard will NOT do this** (switchboard stops reading the transcript before the CLI writes its accounting), so do not test that path expecting it to work. **(4)** THE RESUME CASE, which is what the review round caught: after (3), resume that same conversation and send one more prompt. The tilde must come **back** — the exact figure was the total as of last time, and it must not sit there frozen while the session spends again. **(5)** `/clear` a session with a cost showing: tokens and cost must both go to zero together, and the cost must not keep the old conversation's number. **(6)** The status bar's workspace total should stay sane throughout and never read `$NaN`. **(7)** A brand-new card should read `$0.00`, not `<$0.01` | Automated: 40 unit tests + 23 mutants across two rounds, incl. a fixture that reconciles our arithmetic against the CLI's own figure exactly. What a machine cannot do: judge whether the `~` / `≥` markers and the three tooltips actually read as honest rather than fussy, and confirm the corrected number matches what `/cost` told you inside the session |
| **The Changes tab stops calling every problem "Not a git repository" (#785)** | **RELEASED in v0.8.90 (2026-09-16) — ready to hand-test.** The easiest trigger is a **damaged repository**, which you can make in ten seconds and throw away: create an empty folder, put a file in it called `.git` (a FILE, not a folder) containing the single line `gitdir: /nope`, open a session on it, and open its **Changes** tab. Expected: *"switchboard couldn't read this project's git — not a git repository: …"*, in the attention colour — **not** "Not a git repository" and **not** "Working tree clean". (What follows the colon depends on your git build: this machine's echoes `/nope`, the CI runners' print `(null)`. Either is correct.) **(2)** THE CONTROL, and do not skip it: open the Changes tab on an ordinary folder that is genuinely not a repository. It must still say plainly **Not a git repository**, in grey, with no reason and no alarm — that is the common case and turning it into a warning would be a worse bug than the one being fixed. **(3)** The card header: on the damaged folder, the little `⎇ branch ·N changed` line must be **absent**, not showing `⎇ ?` with no changes. **(4)** If you have a project using **Git Large File Storage installed for that project only** (`git lfs install --local`), open its Changes tab — this used to be the documented case that read as "not a repository", and should now quote git's own complaint about the filter. **(5)** The bus half: ask one session to look at a **damaged** sibling's changes.
